Fintech startup Payhawk valued at $2B in $280M round

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Payhawk Ltd., a startup that helps enterprises manage their business expenses, has raised $280 million in funding.

The Series C round was led by Goldman Sachs’ growth equity arm. The bank was joined by Greenoaks, Lightspeed and more than a half-dozen others. Payhawk stated today that it’s now valued at $2 billion.

Issuing corporate credit cards to employees historically involved a weeks-long wait. Payhawk operates a platform that enables companies to create virtual cards in a few seconds and issue physical ones in five days. According to the company, customers can configure a card to expire automatically at the end of the project for which it’s needed.

Accounting teams can set spending limits to avoid cost overruns. Payhawk makes it possible to tie purchase caps to a business project, a specific merchant or a broad product category.

Companies can optionally connect their cards to Payhawk-operated business accounts. They support payments in more than 115 currencies. According to the software maker, its accounts’ currency exchange rates are up to 90% lower than those charged by traditional banks.

Syncing corporate card spending to a company’s accounting ledger is a multi-step process. First, workers must scan the receipt issued in connection with a purchase and send it to the finance team. From there, an accountant categorizes the purchase and verifies it through a process known as reconciliation.

Payhawk automates the workflow using an artificial intelligence agent. Additionally, it flags cost-cutting opportunities such as unnecessary software-as-a-service subscriptions.

A second AI agent speeds up procurement initiatives. It’s accessible through a chat interface that enables workers to request items in natural language. If a request is missing certain details, the agent can point out what additional information is needed. From there, it automatically seeks approvals from relevant executives.

Payhawk also automates a number of other tasks. It includes a tool that reduces the amount of work involved in changing business trip plans. Additionally, the company has built an AI engine that enables new users to customize its platform by uploading documents such as spending policies.

“Through our technology, finance departments can benefit from ‘zero-touch finance’, where neither the finance professional nor the employee deals with any admin work,” said Payhawk co-founder and Chief Executive Officer Hristo Borisov. “77% of our customers use our agents daily across financial control, procurement, travel and reporting to save time and money for their businesses.”

Payhawk says that it still has 70% of its previous funding round, a $100 million investment that closed in 2022. The company will use the proceeds from today’s Series C raise to enhance its AI capabilities.

Scale the Model, Scale the Harness: Unlocking a 2 Leap in Mathematical Reasoning

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Originally published in Generative AI on Medium.

I spent months building a system that wraps a language model in a checking loop so it can prove hard math theorems. Every step gets verified with real math tools, the system remembers what worked, and a second pass tries to break the proof before it counts.

Then I tested it on 300 hard math problems, and the results surprised me: the setup around the model matters as much as the model itself.

The honest headline is the last column: the loop fixed 16–33% of the problems each model got wrong on its own. Flash is the best model and improves least in multiples (1.64x, not 2x) but most in failures rescued.

The detail I'm proudest of: the local 27B catches factor-of-2 and boundary-case errors both big models miss. Five problems only Qwen solved; it beat Muse on 14 problems and Flash on 10. Flash is also 7.8x faster than Muse at the median solve (12.2 vs 95.0 minutes).

Proofs are graded by our own calibrated LLM judge ensemble, running in an isolated context — no human grading, no formal verification. All the eval logs are public so anyone can recount: certified counts reproduce exactly from the judgment files (Qwen 80, Muse 139, Flash 166).

The full article is published in Generative AI on Medium: https://generativeai.pub/scale-the-model-scale-the-harness-unlocking-a-2-leap-in-mathematical-reasoning-231314ab3ea7

The code and all test results are open: https://github.com/dionyziuz/proof_harness — eval logs: https://github.com/dionyziuz/proof_harness/tree/main/data/eval_results

If you try it or spot a flaw in the method, I'd like to hear it — the comments here or an issue on the repo.

MegaRobo Raises Nearly US$200 Million in New Funding to Build Intelligence Infrastructure for the Physical World

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI's Next Frontier Is the Physical World. Science Is Where It Begins.

SUZHOU, China, Nov. 18, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- MegaRobo Technologies (MegaRobo), a technology company building Intelligence Infrastructure for the Physical World, today announced that it has raised nearly US$200 million in a new financing round joined by Oman Investment Authority (OIA), the sovereign wealth fund of the Sultanate of Oman. And existing investors Asia Investment Capital, Granite Asia and Sinovation Ventures continued to back the company, with new participation from CMB International.

The new financing will support MegaRobo's continued investment in core technology, the global expansion of its AI for Science business, and the development of infrastructure that enables AI to operate in increasingly complex physical environments.

The first generation of AI learned primarily from digital information - text, images, video and code. The next generation will increasingly learn by interacting with the physical world: acting on real systems, observing outcomes, and using those outcomes to decide what to do next.

In science, that means moving beyond reading papers, predicting structures or generating molecules. AI must also be able to design experiments, execute them in real laboratories, observe the results and learn from what happens.

MegaRobo is building the infrastructure for that transition. Its proprietary One Intelligence Stack connects intelligence with execution - bringing together models and agents, data, workflows, software, scientific instruments, robotics, automation and real-world feedback in a single reusable technology foundation.

One Intelligence Stack. Across Complex Physical Systems.THINK  â  DESIGN  â  EXECUTE  â  OBSERVE  â  LEARN

Science is the first proving ground for this architecture because it requires AI to do more than analyze information. Scientific discovery depends on an ongoing loop between reasoning, design, physical experimentation, observation and learning - often under conditions where the answer is not known in advance.

MegaRobo is applying this architecture to scientific discovery and pharmaceutical R&D through an integrated AI for Science infrastructure that combines AI, scientific computing, experimental execution, data and R&D decision-making.

The company has delivered products and technologies to more than 500 customers, including leading global pharmaceutical companies, drug discovery organizations, biotechnology companies and research institutions. Its systems support key stages of discovery and development, including early research, target and mechanism discovery, drug screening and candidate optimization.

Through Megalaxy Lab, MegaRobo is also making real-world experimentation increasingly programmable. Researchers and AI agents can configure experiments, route them to automated laboratory systems, track execution, and receive structured, machine-readable results that can feed directly into the next cycle of scientific reasoning. Megalaxy Lab has served more than 200 customers.

MegaRobo is now extending this capability to the global AI for Science community. Together with industry partners including Tencent Cloud and Twist Bioscience, the company has launched Poreva Open, a global AI protein design challenge open to all models and technical approaches. Megalaxy Lab will provide wet-lab validation for more than 10,000 protein design sequences, creating an opportunity for innovators worldwide to test their models against real experimental results. By opening up large-scale experimental validation to the global research community, Poreva Open aims to help establish a common benchmark for AI-driven protein design.

This marks a shift in how scientific infrastructure is accessed: from laboratory work organized mainly as individual projects toward experimental capacity that can increasingly be standardized, automated and called on demand by scientists, software and AI agents.

A Reusable Foundation for Complex Physical Systems

Science is where MegaRobo is starting, but the underlying architecture is designed for other complex physical systems as well. Wherever AI must interact with instruments, machines, materials, processes and real-world feedback, the same core challenge appears: intelligence must connect to execution, and execution must generate observations that intelligence can learn from.

MegaRobo is developing One Intelligence Stack as a common technology foundation for this interaction - not as a collection of isolated AI applications or automation products, but as infrastructure that enables AI to act, observe and learn across complex physical environments.

Daniel Huang, Founder and CEO of MegaRobo, said: "The first generation of AI learned from humanity's accumulated knowledge. The next generation will increasingly learn by acting in the physical world - through experiments, production and continuous feedback. That shift changes AI from a system that interprets the world into one that can help create new knowledge, materials, processes and products. MegaRobo is building the infrastructure for that transition."

Liu Erh Fei, Founding Partner and CEO of Asia Investment Capital, said:"As a long-term shareholder, we have seen MegaRobo continuously drive innovation, earn the trust of its customers and translate its technological capabilities into commercial applications. Its ability to integrate AI, software and hardware, together with its experience in scientific research and advanced manufacturing, underpins our continued investment. We look forward to continuing to support MegaRobo as it further strengthens its product and technology platforms and serves more customers worldwide."

The new round builds on MegaRobo's long-standing backing from leading global investors and strategic partners. To date, the company has raised approximately US$600 million across multiple financing rounds. This includes a nearly US$300 million Series C round co-led by Goldman Sachs Alternatives, Asia Investment Capital and Granite Asia, with participation from Pavilion Capital, a part of Temasek's Seviora Group. Long-term investors such as Sinovation Ventures, Joy Capital and MPCi have continued to support MegaRobo through multiple stages of its growth. Earlier industry investors also include WuXi AppTec and Bosch Ventures.

Together, this diverse group of technology, financial and industrial partners reflect MegaRobo's evolution into a global technology company with deep capabilities spanning scientific discovery and complex physical systems. Its continued expansion across North America, Europe, the Middle East, and Asia will accelerate the global scale-up of intelligence infrastructure for the physical world.

How IP Intelligence Helps Catch Account Takeovers in Real Time

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A correct password no longer proves that the right person is logging in. Leaked credentials are traded in huge lists, and attackers use automated tools to try them across thousands of websites. When one works, the login looks completely normal: right username, right password, no failed attempts.

So how do you tell a real customer from an attacker holding their password? Look at where the login is coming from.

The login looks right, but the connection doesn’t

Every login request comes with an IP address, and that address carries context. With an IP intelligence lookup at sign-in, you can compare each attempt against what’s normal for that user and spot the outliers.

Unexpected location. A customer who always logs in from Hanoi suddenly appears in another country an hour later. IP geolocation flags this kind of “impossible travel” right away.

Unusual network. Real users log in from home Wi-Fi or a mobile carrier. An ASN lookup shows when a login comes from a hosting provider instead, and datacenter detection confirms it. That’s a common sign of automated credential-stuffing tools.

Hidden identity. Attackers often route their traffic through VPNs, proxies or Tor to avoid being traced or rate-limited. Detecting these doesn’t mean blocking every user who values privacy. It means adding a second check when other signals also look off.

Known bad actors. If an IP already has a history of abuse, such as spam, scanning or past attacks, a login from that address deserves extra scrutiny no matter how valid the password is.

The goal isn’t to lock people out. It’s to match your response to the risk. A familiar location on a home network can go straight through. A new country through a datacenter IP with abuse history might trigger a one-time code or an email confirmation. Real users barely notice, and attackers get stopped at the door.

HideIP is an IP Intelligence API that returns geolocation, ASN, VPN/proxy/Tor detection, datacenter identification and abuse history in a single call. Adding it to your login flow gives your security logic the context it needs to catch account takeovers before any damage is done.

How to Build a Desktop Barcode Scanner with Tauri and Rust

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 19 • Originally published at dynamsoft.com

Tauri's official barcode-scanner plugin only runs on Android and iOS — it has no desktop implementation at all. If you are building a Tauri app for Windows, macOS, or Linux and need to read barcodes, the official plugin cannot help you. This tutorial builds the desktop scanner that fills that gap: a Tauri 2 app whose Rust backend decodes image files, PDFs, and a live webcam feed through FFI bindings to the Dynamsoft Barcode Reader SDK, while the UI is plain HTML/CSS/JS rendered in the system webview. The result decodes every barcode type the SDK supports, draws a quadrilateral over each one, and lists its format, text, and corner coordinates. Multi-page PDFs and TIFFs are previewed page by page, with on-screen arrows and left/right keys to switch pages.

The finished app decoding 32 ITF barcodes from a single photo:

See it running — opening an image, decoding, and switching to the live camera:

What you'll build: A cross-platform Tauri 2 desktop barcode scanner with a Rust backend and a no-bundler web frontend. It opens images/PDFs through a native file dialog or a typed path, previews each document page with a cyan overlay on every decoded barcode, lists each barcode's format/text/coordinates, pages through multi-page PDFs and TIFFs, and streams a live webcam feed (Windows) — all powered by the same Dynamsoft Barcode Reader C++ core used by the Slint version of this sample.

This is the central reason this sample exists. Tauri ships an official barcode-scanner plugin, and its name suggests it is the obvious choice for any Tauri barcode app. It is not — at least not on desktop.

The plugin's documentation describes it as a way to "allow your mobile application to use the camera to scan QR codes, EAN-13 and other kinds of barcodes." Three details in the official setup confirm the desktop gap:

On Windows, macOS, and Linux there is simply no implementation to call. Beyond the platform gap, the plugin is also camera-only: it opens a native scan view (its windowed: true mode makes the webview transparent so the camera shows through) and returns what the camera sees. It does not decode a photo you already have on disk, does not read PDFs, and is not designed to return a batch of barcodes with precise corner coordinates from a still image.

This sample complements the official plugin rather than competing with it on mobile. It covers the desktop platforms the plugin leaves out, and adds the file/PDF decoding and multi-barcode coordinate output that a live mobile scan view does not provide:

The practical takeaway: if your Tauri app targets desktop, or needs to read barcodes from files rather than only from a live mobile camera, you need a native decoding backend. The rest of this article builds one with Rust and the Dynamsoft Barcode Reader SDK, and shows how to wire it into a Tauri webview UI.

The sample repository already contains a Rust CLI and a Slint desktop GUI that share a native decoding core. The Tauri app is a third frontend on that same core. Nothing about the decoding, licensing, or camera capture is rewritten — the Tauri crate literally compiles the same source files:

Only the presentation layer differs. The webview and the Rust backend talk over three channels, each suited to a different kind of traffic:

The project layout is small enough to read in one sitting:

The crate depends on Tauri 2, the dialog plugin, serde for JSON, and the image crate for reading and re-encoding pictures. There is no frontend framework dependency:

tauri.conf.json points the webview at the static frontend, enables the global Tauri API, defines the window, and sets a Content Security Policy:

Two settings do the heavy lifting for the "no bundler" design:

The CSP allows the media: scheme (and its Windows form http://media.localhost) for images, and ipc: (plus http://ipc.localhost) for Tauri's own command channel. Keep these in sync with the protocol you register in Step 5, or the webview will silently block the pixels.

build.rs has two jobs: generate the Tauri context, and compile/link the native Dynamsoft core. The Tauri part is a single call at the end; the rest is ordinary cc + linker configuration that mirrors the Slint example's build.

On Windows the SDK ships as static import libraries plus runtime DLLs. The script links the five Dynamsoft libraries statically, links litecam dynamically, and copies every DLL next to the executable:

Linux and macOS link DynamsoftBarcodeReader as a dynamic library and set an rpath so the binary finds the .so/.dylib at runtime. copy_resources copies the SDK's Templates/ and Models/ folders beside the executable, because the SDK resolves them at runtime from the module directory.

The C ABI bridge is compiled with cc and shared with the Slint example — this is the "one native core" promise made concrete:

Two helpers are worth copying into your own Tauri + native projects:

profile_dir() is how the script knows where to drop the DLLs so they sit beside the final executable. absolute() strips the Windows \\?\ verbatim prefix that canonicalize() adds, because the MSVC linker rejects it. copy_file deliberately skips a destination that already exists — a running instance keeps its DLLs locked, and the files are identical anyway.

The decoding core is a flat C API over the Dynamsoft Capture Vision Router. The C++ side (lib/bridge.cpp) wraps CCaptureVisionRouter and returns malloc'd structs; the Rust side (src/scanner.rs) declares them with #[repr(C)] and frees them after copying.

The buffer entry point is the one that matters for coordinate alignment. It wraps the raw bytes in a CImageData tagged IPF_RGB_888 and captures with the read-barcodes preset:

Two file entry points sit on the bridge. decode_barcode_file is the flat API used by the CLI and Slint UI: it calls CaptureMultiPages and concatenates every page's barcodes into one list. The Tauri GUI uses decode_barcode_file_pages because it also needs the pixels of each page for the preview.

The router does not keep page pixels around by default — GetOriginalImage returns NULL unless the original image was cached — and CaptureMultiPages only hands back decoded items. The SDK's CFileFetcher is the documented tool for this job: it partitions a multi-page PDF or TIFF into one CImageData per page. The bridge loops over those page bitmaps, copies each into RGB888, and decodes that exact bitmap with Capture():

image_to_rgb copies a CImageData into a tightly packed RGB buffer, honoring GetStride() and converting the formats the rasterizer produces — RGB/BGR 888, grayscale, 8-bit binary, and ARGB/ABGR 8888. One gotcha: on this SDK build CFileFetcher hands back BGR bytes even though GetImagePixelFormat() reports IPF_RGB_888, so the 3-byte converter always swaps red and blue. A red/green/blue test image is the quickest way to catch a channel-order mistake. Because every page keeps its own pixels, the pager can switch pages later without touching the file again.

append_barcode_items reads each item's CQuadrilateral into four corner points and copies the format and text strings; the packing loop hands ownership of those strings to the returned PageResults. An EXIF-rotated photo simply becomes a one-page document, so the same code path covers images, PDFs, and TIFFs. In this sample, images/MultiPage.pdf becomes 4 pages with one QR code each.

For the buffer path, extract_barcodes reads each item's CQuadrilateral location into the four corner points, copies the format and text strings, and releases the SDK result. On the Rust side, the safe wrapper initializes the license once, creates the instance, and frees it on Drop:

collect_results and collect_pages copy every native struct into owned Rust values and then call free_barcode / free_page_results, so no native memory outlives the call. Because the Tauri crate includes this exact file with #[path], the desktop app and the Slint app decode byte-for-byte identically.

main.rs builds the Tauri app: it creates the reader, registers the dialog plugin, manages shared state, registers the media: protocol, and lists the commands the webview may call.

Three design points are worth understanding:

Tauri injects this state into any command that asks for State, which is how the command layer reaches the reader and the media store without globals.

Commands are Rust functions marked #[tauri::command] and listed in invoke_handler. Arguments and return values are serialized with serde. The result types use camelCase so the JSON is idiomatic on the JS side:

The tagged enum DecodeReply lets the frontend distinguish "show a message" from "here are results" by reading reply.kind.

startup_status reports a license problem once, before the first decode:

open_image is async because a modal file dialog must not block the thread that pumps window messages. It uses the dialog plugin's callback API plus a one-shot channel:

The other commands stay synchronous. Tauri runs sync commands on the main thread, which keeps the native Capture Vision handle on a single thread — the same thread-affinity contract the Slint UI relies on. The camera worker (Step 9) owns a second reader instance for its own thread, so the two never share a handle.

decode_path is the heart of the app. It stops the camera, validates the path, asks the bridge for one entry per page, and stores each page's pixels together with its barcodes:

set_page switches the preview to another page without decoding the file again — every page is already in MediaStore:

The coordinate-space decision is deliberate. The SDK rasterizes a page and the bridge decodes the same bitmap it returns for the preview, so overlay points and the displayed image always share one coordinate space — whether the source is an EXIF-rotated phone photo or page 3 of a PDF. Preview bytes are encoded lazily in the next step, so decoding a 4-page 2481×3509 document stays fast.

Sending a 1280×720 frame as base64 through the IPC channel on every camera tick would waste megabytes a second. Instead, Rust keeps the loaded pages and the latest camera JPEG in a MediaStore and serves them over a registered URI scheme. Pages are stored as raw RGB and JPEG-encoded on first view, then cached:

Lazy encoding matters for multi-page documents. A 2481×3509 PDF page is about 26 MB of raw RGB, and eagerly PNG-encoding four of them in a debug build blocked the UI for seconds. JPEG-encoding one page on demand takes a fraction of that time, and dropping the raw buffer afterward keeps memory bounded. When the pager moves to a new page, the requests the same path with a new seq, so the handler encodes that page once and caches it. The camera path stays separate and keeps pushing one JPEG per frame.

The handler is registered in main.rs with .register_uri_scheme_protocol("media", media::handle). The frontend builds a platform-correct URL with convertFileSrc:

convertFileSrc("camera.jpg", "media") produces http://media.localhost/camera.jpg on Windows and media://localhost/camera.jpg elsewhere. The seq counter is a cache-buster: each new page or frame increments it, so the always fetches fresh bytes. The payload travels as a normal HTTP response with Cache-Control: no-store, not as JSON — which is exactly why this channel exists separately from commands and events.

The frontend is three static files. index.html is the toolbar, preview pane, results list, and status bar:

The script is type="module" because app.js uses top-level await for listen and startup_status. app.js grabs the Tauri API from the injected global and wires the buttons to commands:

The pager stays out of the way for single-page images and the camera, and only appears when the reply reports more than one page:

Because set_page only swaps the current index in MediaStore, switching pages is instant after the first view of that page. The arrow keys call the same gotoPage path; the handler ignores them while the path input has focus so the caret still moves normally.

The results list is built with textContent, never innerHTML, because barcode text is untrusted input that could contain markup:

One CSS detail matters for the preview. The placeholder uses display: flex, which would override the browser's default [hidden] { display: none } rule, so the stylesheet forces it:

Without this, hiding the placeholder or the stage with the hidden attribute would not actually hide it.

The overlay is a stacked on the . The trick that keeps coordinates simple: give the canvas the image's own pixel size, then stretch both to the same letterboxed rectangle. The canvas coordinate space then equals the image coordinate space, so barcode points are drawn without conversion.

showPreview sets view.imageReady = false before it swaps the source (file pages pass waitForLoad = true). layout() still sizes the stage and clears the canvas immediately, but drawOverlay returns until the load event fires. At that point the new page is visible and the boxes are drawn. Camera frames skip the wait, because the previous frame is already on screen and the stream replaces it every ~33 ms.

layout() computes a contain-fit rectangle (the smaller of the width/height ratios) and centers it. The canvas backing store is set to view.width × view.height — the image's intrinsic pixels — while CSS stretches it to the letterboxed size. The only scale-dependent value is lineWidth = 2 / scale, which keeps the outline about two screen pixels regardless of zoom, matching the Slint version. A ResizeObserver re-runs layout() whenever the pane resizes:

The CSS that stacks the image and canvas in the same box:

Because the decoder ran on the same per-page bitmap that became the preview (Step 3 and Step 5), the points line up with the displayed pixels even for EXIF-rotated photos — no rotation or offset math is needed here. The overlay draws only the outline; a translucent fill over a dense QR code reads as a colour shift when the preview is scaled down.

The camera is native, not the webview's getUserMedia. On Windows the bundled litecam library captures from Media Foundation and delivers RGB frames through a C ABI. The Rust wrapper copies each frame out and releases the native buffer:

start_camera spawns a worker thread that owns its own reader instance, so the native handle stays on that thread. The loop grabs a frame, caps itself near 30 fps, decodes, encodes JPEG, stores it, and emits an event:

The frontend listens for the event and repaints the same preview/overlay/results path used for still images:

Camera frames hide the pager (view.pages = 0) because the live stream is a single surface. If no frame arrives within three seconds, the worker emits camera-error instead of leaving the status on "Starting camera..." — almost always because another app (a browser tab using getUserMedia, a video call, or a second scanner instance) holds the device.

This is the event channel doing what events are for: pushing data the webview did not explicitly request. The seq in each payload tells the which JPEG to fetch from the media: protocol. On non-Windows platforms start_camera returns a clear error ("Camera capture is only wired for Windows in this example."), while image and PDF decoding still work everywhere.

Tauri blocks every potentially dangerous command by default; you grant access explicitly in a capability file. This app grants the main window only core:default (invoke + events):

Notice what is not granted: no dialog permission. The file picker is opened from Rust (open_image calls app.dialog()), so the webview never needs the ability to open dialogs itself. This is the principle of least privilege applied to the IPC surface — the webview can call your five commands and listen to events, and nothing else.

The CSP in tauri.conf.json (Step 1) restricts where images and connections may come from, and the results list uses textContent (Step 7) so untrusted barcode text cannot inject markup. Together these keep the webview side safe even though it renders data decoded from arbitrary files.

With the SDK and litecam vendored in the repo, building is a single Cargo command — no Node.js, no bundler, no npm install:

On Windows you can double-click tauri/run.bat. The first build compiles the Dynamsoft bridge and links the SDK, so it takes longer than later builds.

If you want the Tauri CLI for dev-server orchestration and installers, it is optional:

Because there is no devUrl, Tauri embeds the frontend assets into the binary in both debug and release. If you edit ui/*.js or ui/*.css, rebuild (or restart cargo run) to see the change — a webview reload alone will not pick up disk edits.

Get the complete sample project source code on GitHub.

Your AI Video Pipeline Is a Distributed System, Not a Magic Button

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A demo makes AI video generation look wonderfully simple: send a prompt, wait a little, receive an MP4.

Then you try to ship the same idea inside a real product.

A user closes the tab halfway through. A render takes longer than expected. The provider succeeds, but your webhook arrives twice. The marketing team changes one sentence and accidentally loses the previous version. A finished video contains the wrong product name, yet the workflow publishes it automatically.

That is the real pain in AI video today. Generating footage is only one part of the job. The harder problem is building a system that can survive waiting, failure, revision, and human judgment.

The most useful mental shift is to stop treating an AI video tool like a synchronous media endpoint. It behaves much more like a distributed build system.

When someone asks for “a 45-second vertical explainer for first-time customers,” the prompt may also imply a script, shot plan, voice, music, captions, timing, rendering, and export. Those parts depend on one another.

Change the narration and the caption timing may move. Shorten a scene and the music cue may no longer land correctly. Replace a product image and the layout may need another render. Even a tiny editorial request can create work across the rest of the pipeline.

This is why a single blocking HTTP request is a poor abstraction. Your application should submit intent, get a durable task identifier, and let the production job continue independently of the browser session.

A small TypeScript client might begin like this:

The important part is not the endpoint name. It is the contract: submission creates a resource. The video does not have to exist yet for the job to exist.

Teams often treat polling as an embarrassing temporary solution. It is not. For long-running media work, explicit polling can be simple, observable, and reliable when it is designed carefully.

The mistake is polling every second forever from a browser. A better design uses bounded retries, backoff, jitter, and a terminal state.

In production, this waiting loop usually belongs in a worker, not in the user's browser. The UI can reconnect whenever it wants and read the latest state from your database. That one decision removes a surprising amount of fragility.

It also makes the interface more honest. Instead of a spinner that says nothing, you can show meaningful phases such as planning, producing, reviewing, and ready. Users are generally comfortable waiting when they can see that the work has not disappeared.

Once a job can outlive a request, duplicate submissions become a real risk.

A user may double-click. Your server may time out after the provider accepted the request. A queue may redeliver a message. If every retry creates a fresh render, you can end up paying for several identical videos while showing only one of them.

Give each logical submission an idempotency key. It can be generated by the client or derived from a stable internal request ID. Store the relationship before starting expensive work.

The database still needs a unique constraint. Application-level checks alone can race under concurrent requests.

This sounds like ordinary backend engineering because it is ordinary backend engineering. AI does not remove the old reliability problems. It adds expensive, slow, probabilistic work to them.

The first render is rarely the final render. Someone will ask for a calmer voice, a shorter opening, a different visual style, or one corrected product detail.

If every change overwrites the same row, debugging becomes painful. You lose the prompt that created the output, the assets used at the time, and the reason one version was accepted while another was rejected.

Treat each revision as a new job with a parent:

Now the history tells a story. A local edit can point to its source render. A stylistic remix can branch without destroying the approved version. An extension can preserve the original video while adding a new ending.

This is useful for more than debugging. It gives reviewers a concrete diff in intent: “change the second scene's title” is very different from “reimagine the whole video as a playful social ad.” Your system can apply different review rules to each operation.

A finished URL is not enough for an operational system.

Keep the original brief, normalized settings, reference asset identifiers, provider task ID, model or workflow version, state transitions, error details, output metadata, and review decision. If the provider returns temporary URLs, copy approved assets into storage you control according to your retention policy.

You will want this information the first time a stakeholder asks why two similar prompts produced different results. You will also want it when a model changes, a render must be reproduced, or an old asset loses its usage rights.

Be careful with logs. Prompts can contain customer data, unreleased product names, or links to private media. Log identifiers and structured events by default; avoid dumping entire requests into a shared logging system.

A render can be technically successful and still be unusable.

Captions may misspell a brand. A generated scene may show the wrong interface. The voice may read a number incorrectly. A reference asset may be used in a way that violates its license. None of those problems necessarily produce an API error.

So keep generation status separate from editorial status.

A practical workflow can automatically check duration, dimensions, audio presence, black frames, and caption boundaries. A person should still review factual accuracy, brand fit, rights, and whether the video is actually worth publishing.

This is not anti-automation. It is how automation becomes trustworthy. Machines can remove repetitive assembly work while people spend their attention on decisions that require context.

The best AI video experience is not the one with the flashiest generate button. It is the one where users can leave and come back, understand what is happening, retry safely, compare revisions, and recover from failure without contacting support.

That experience comes from a few unglamorous choices: durable job records, explicit states, idempotent submission, bounded polling, immutable revisions, careful metadata, and a review gate before distribution.

Once those pieces exist, the model behind the pipeline becomes easier to change. You can improve generation quality, add another provider, or introduce a new editing mode without rebuilding the operational foundation.

The prompt may start the video, but the job system is what makes it a product.

Who called me about free Netflix? Treat an inbound one-time code as a bearer token

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

“Who called me about free Netflix?” is easy to file as a sales annoyance. If a one-time code from a text message was read back during the same call, the more precise problem is a credential-handling one: an inbound voice path was used as if it were a bound channel for a bearer token.

Affiliation and AI note: This is promotional editorial from the website whocallme.co.uk, drafted with AI assistance. The worksheet below is a manual record design. It is not a deployed tool, a laboratory test, or a claim that the author contacted EE or Netflix.

Our page for 020 3807 0999 is presented as a landline, with an allocation hint of DIDWW Ireland Limited and a numbering area of London. Those labels do not identify the caller or a physical origin for the call. The page shows 6 searches and one user report. A “first seen” label of “4 days ago” is a page label, not the time the comment was written, and not a reason to describe the note as new this week.

The note is imported, and its original publication date is unknown. It carries a visitor category of Scam/Fraud. That category is a visitor label, not an independent finding about the number holder.

The visitor wrote that someone was “Pretending to be from EE”, “said I was getting free promotional netflix”, and that the visitor “gave them a code texted to me from Netflix”. The visitor “got suspicious when they asked me for my address”, “hung up and contacted both EE and Netflix directly who confirmed it was a scam and secured my accounts for me, saying i wouldnt be charged”.

I did not contact EE or Netflix, and I did not reproduce the call. The statements about confirmation, account securing, and the absence of a charge belong to the visitor’s account. They are not official conclusions I can restate as established facts, and they are not billing results I have inspected.

The public note about a promotional-code call is anonymous and unverified. A displayed caller ID can be spoofed. The named companies remain part of the visitor’s description. That is still enough material for an incident-record discussion, because the note already separates three events: a code was disclosed, later contact was made through channels the visitor chose, and the identity of the inbound caller was treated as untrustworthy.

In access-control terms, a one-time password or SMS code is usually a bearer instrument for a short window. The service that issued the code typically cannot see who is sitting next to the handset. Whoever presents the code may complete the action the code was meant to authorise: a login, a recovery step, a subscription change, or another account operation. This article does not claim to know which action, if any, was attempted in the visitor’s case. The engineering point is the property of the code, not a reconstructed attack path.

Reading the code aloud to an inbound caller is functionally close to pasting it into a form you do not control. The voice call feels like a conversation with a company. The code still travels as a secret that confers whatever power the issuer attached to it. Once disclosed, the useful record is that disclosure happened, not a copy of the digits. Do not store the code in a ticket.

A later question for an address, which is what made the visitor suspicious, is a separate request. It may indicate that the caller wanted more data. It does not tell us whether the code had already been used. The record should not invent a successful or failed redemption.

A bound channel, in this narrow sense, is a session or address that the account holder can recognise from a starting point they already control: an official app already signed in, a URL they typed from a known domain, a number printed on a bill they already hold. An inbound voice call does not become that bound channel merely because the speaker uses a company name, or because a text message from a familiar brand arrives during the same conversation.

SMS delivery of a code proves, at most, that a message reached a number the issuer had on file, or that a message appeared to come from a familiar sender. It does not bind the person currently speaking on the other line to that issuer. The inbound caller and the SMS are two channels. Mixing them — reading the SMS into the call — gives the unbound voice path the token.

This is why “they already sounded like EE” is not a channel check. The visitor later chose to contact EE and Netflix directly. In a record, that later choice should be written as official-channel contact reported by the visitor, not as proof automatically attached to the original inbound number.

The table below is a worksheet you can copy into a blank document. No telemetry, packet capture, or live test is required. Do not add fields for a password, a full address, or the code itself.

The example column is a manual illustration using the published wording. It is not a newly observed incident at your organisation, and it is not a charge record. If your form requires a date, add a review date that is clearly the date you filled the worksheet. Do not copy the page’s first-seen label into an incident-time field.

The fact that a code was disclosed is one decision point: secrets may have been exposed, so the account holder may want to use an official channel they already trust. The fact that the visitor later contacted EE and Netflix is a second decision point: that contact, if it occurred as written, is about account state, not about proving who the inbound caller was. The identity of the inbound caller is a third decision, and it can remain unknown without blocking the first two.

Human review is the right point to stop enrichment. A reviewer can check that the code itself was not pasted into the ticket, that no address was added “for completeness”, and that company names stay in the claimed_organisation field rather than in a vendor-ID field. If the reviewer was not a party to the call, they should not invent a test call in order to complete the form.

A collection of published UK telephone reports can supply wording for a source pointer when the source is a public page. It cannot bind an inbound caller to a brand, confirm a subscription charge, or replace the official channel the account holder already uses. Leave the caller’s identity unresolved if the evidence does not resolve it. The useful end state of this worksheet is a short, attributed record: a token was disclosed, later official contact was reported, and the inbound speaker remains unverified.

The 8 Best-Performed Audiobooks of All Time, Ranked

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The concept of audiobooks was born alongside Thomas Edison's phonograph in the late 19th century, but actual "Talking Books" as a commercial category appeared in the 1930s as a tool for visually impaired readers without knowledge of or access to Braille to experience literature. In the years since, the medium has evolved greatly alongside technology like cassette tapes and CDs. Now, in the digital era, audiobooks have become such an abundant and mainstream product that even people with no kind of visual impairment have come to choose them as their preferred method of reading.

But while several incredible audiobooks have been produced over the years, a special few can easily be counted among the best, most vividly performed in the medium's history. A truly great narrator feels like they have a genuinely profound connection to the material, delivering each line with appropriate emotional weight and making it easier to understand the subtext of the original book. If they portray each character's voice with a distinct characterization, pace their cadence well, and fit the book's genre well, any narrator can make their audiobook performance go down in history as legendary.

Some of its narrative elements haven't aged perfectly, but in no way does that detract from the fact that Harper Lee's To Kill a Mockingbird is one of the greatest and most foundational American books ever written about racial prejudice, systemic injustice, and human nature. It was the novel that earned Lee a Pulitzer Prize in 1961, and it has aged wonderfully as one of the most influential Southern Gothic novels of the entire 20th century. Who better to narrate such a quintessential piece of Southern American literature than legendary Texas native Sissy Spacek?

Through sheer vocal authenticity and dramatic restraint, Spacek makes this one of the best audiobooks of all time. For one, the actress effectively uses her Southern drawl and cadence to make the small-town Alabama setting feel all the more immersive, elevating Lee's beautiful prose to make it feel all the more melodic. She also perfectly captures the spirit and personality of every character, with the way she marks the contrast between child Scout and adult Scout standing out as a particularly memorable narrative choice.

Where many of the best-narrated audiobooks in history were one-person shows, the Lincoln in the Bardo audiobook stands out as perhaps the greatest audiobook ever narrated by an ensemble cast. Written by George Saunders, this 2017 experimental novel is narrated by a shifting crowd of 166 spirits who are often unaware that they are dead. It's full of wonderfully surreal magical realism, making it such a special book that Saunders, who usually recorded his audiobooks himself, decided that he needed a full cast in order to do the text justice.

Saunders' friend Nick Offerman and his wife, Megam Mullally, agreed to perform in the audiobook cast and recruit a bunch of other narrators, including celebrities like Don Cheadle and non-celebrities like Saunders' family and friends. With a gargantuan 166-person cast, Lincoln in the Bardo easily takes the cake as the audiobook with the biggest narrator ensemble in history, and the resulting product feels surprisingly even and creatively coherent. With such different voices appearing over the course of seven and a half hours, the book's rhythmic prose, hypnotic pacing, and sense of humor are all elevated.

The Lord of the Rings began with J. R. R. Tolkien's publisher's request that he write a sequel to the mega-successful children's fantasy book The Hobbit. What started as more of a simple follow-up gradually started transforming into something far bigger and more ambitious, until we finally got what many would confidently call the most important and influential fantasy novel of the 20th century. Indeed, though it was published in three volumes between 1954 and 1955, The Lord of the Rings is technically all one huge fantasy epic, easily one of the most ambitious high fantasy books of all time.

After his participation in Peter Jackson's Middle-earth film trilogy, it was Andy Serkis who narrated the second-ever unabridged audiobook edition of the novel, also published in three separate volumes. Clocking in at roughly 65 hours, his take on this towering achievement of fantasy storytelling brings a unique and unforgettable voice not only to Gollum, but to every other character that takes part in Frodo's journey. Serkis also fully commits to the many songs and poems that Tolkien sprinkled across the novel, adding further vocal variety to an audiobook that was already exquisitely varied.

British author Joe Abercrombie's The First Law series is one of the greatest pieces of grimdark fantasy of the 21st century, composed of two trilogies, three standalone novels, and two short story collections. The book that started it all was The Blade Itself, the first chapter in the trilogy that's also titled The First Law. While it's undoubtedly one of the most essential dark fantasy masterpieces of the last 20 years, it's usually agreed upon among fans that several of its sequels are even better. When it comes to audiobook versions, though, there's no beating the original.

English actor and stage performer Steven Pacey, known for his work in shows like Blake's 7 and MobLand, narrates the Blade Itself audiobook with precisely the kind of gritty realism and razor-sharp comedic timing that a masterpiece like this would need. It's a masterclass in voice control where Pacey switches between the dramatically distinct voice of each character with the utmost ease, as well as a masterclass in dry British wit that complements Abercrombie's dark sense of humor flawlessly. As the book moves quickly from brutal action to slow-burning political scheming to moments of pure dark comedy, Pacey matches the rhythm of such transitions perfectly.

There can be no conversation about the best-narrated audiobooks in history without talking about the best-narrated biographies and autobiographies. Readers may point to many different books when asked about what the best-narrated autobiography of all time is, but there's one title that's bound to come up more often than most: Born a Crime, the comedic autobiography of South African comedian, writer, and political commentator Trevor Noah.

The book is primarily focused on Noah's childhood in South Africa after he was born to an illegal interracial relationship during the apartheid era, and this audiobook version has long been regarded as the gold standard of non-fiction narrated by the author. As a natural polyglot, a seasoned stand-up comedian, and an expert speaker thanks to his experience as a television host, Noah narrates Born a Crime not only with the deepest emotional intimacy possible, but also with a level of cultural authenticity that feels essential to the book.

J. K. Rowling's Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone (published in the United States as Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone) took a couple of years to become a true commercial juggernaut, kicking off the most financially successful fantasy book series of all time. As such, it was entirely unsurprising when Harry Potter and the Deathly Hallows broke the record as the book with the most volumes sold within 24 hours of release, instantly cementing itself as one of the best books of the 2000s.

Audiobook editions were released simultaneously on the day of the book's publication, with the UK edition being narrated by Stephen Fry and the US edition being narrated by Jim Dale. While both Fry and Dale broke the record for the most distinct character voices recorded by an individual on an audiobook at the time (with a whopping 146), there's something special about Dale's performance that earned him the 2008 Grammy for the Best Spoken Word Album for Children. Theatrical, energetic, and full of emotionally cathartic moments, Dale's narration is an all-timer.

The LitRPG genre is a category of speculative fiction where the story explicitly incorporates mechanics and tropes from video games and tabletop RPGs into the narrative, and as far as LitRPG novels go, it doesn't get any better than Matt Dinniman's Dungeon Crawler Carl series. The series was initially self-published until it was acquired by Ace Books in 2024. The first of the bunch is 2020's Dungeon Crawler Carl, one of those sci-fi and fantasy books that are perfect from start to finish.

Dinniman's studio, Soundbooth Theater, produced an audiobook of Dungeon Crawler Carl in 2021, narrated by voice artist Jeff Hays. Widely cited as a rare case where the audiobook edition of a book practically eclipses the print version, the Dungeon Crawler Carl audiobook is anchored by Hays' jaw-dropping vocal range, at times making it feel like you're listening to a full cast rather than a single performer. Seamlessly paced and full of humor, Hays' narration is absolute perfection.

Andy Weir has only published four novels so far in his career, but they have already been enough for him to establish and cement himself as one of the leading hard sci-fi authors currently working. His latest work is Project Hail Mary, widely praised as his magnum opus and one of those books that are a masterclass in hard sci-fi writing. The winner of the 2022 Audie Award for Audiobook of the Year, Ray Porter's unabridged audiobook has come to be praised by many as the single best-performed audiobook of all time.

After listening to Porter's performance once, it's hard to imagine ever revisiting Project Hail Mary in any other way again. It's the definitive way to experience the story, not only because the medium couples perfectly with the novel's heavy focus on themes of communication. Porter captures both Grace and Rocky's voices faultlessly, and he manages to turn the many complex scientific elements into exposition that feels surprisingly conversational and humorous. Energetic, well-paced, emotional, and tonally varied, it's some of the greatest narration that any audiobook has ever delivered.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Anthropic Priced Its IPO at $83. Here's What I'd Pay for the Stock.

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic's explosive growth has made its IPO one of the biggest events in AI. But its enormous valuation could limit investors' future returns.

By Matt DiLallo – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic, PBC ›

Anthropic's (ANT +0.00%) initial public offering (IPO) is one of the biggest events in the AI industry. The developer of Claude has grown its revenue at an extraordinary pace as businesses adopt its AI tools. The AI giant just priced its IPO at $83 per share, above its expected range of $70 to $78 per share.

However, that price already assumes considerable success. Even if Anthropic becomes one of the world's most successful AI companies, its stock could still deliver disappointing returns from here.

I ran the numbers on what Anthropic's stock could be worth over the next five and 10 years. Here's what they suggest, and the price I'd be willing to pay for the AI stock.

Anthropic recorded $33.2 billion in revenue in just the first nine months of 2026, up a blistering 1,200% compared to the same period last year. Its third-quarter revenue alone was around $17 billion, and its annualized revenue run rate reached $81 billion by Oct. 31, up $10 billion from the prior month.

Enterprise adoption of Claude, its coding tools, API usage, and integrations give Anthropic several avenues for continued growth. However, AI infrastructure and model training are extraordinarily expensive. Anthropic committed to spending a staggering $895.4 billion on compute and infrastructure via purchase commitments and lease agreements. Meanwhile, Anthropic's revenue growth will also almost certainly slow as its business scales.

At $83 per share, Anthropic has a valuation of about $1.5 trillion. That's around 18 times its $81 billion run rate.

That run rate isn't the same as reported annual sales. It extrapolates recent results, which inflates a company growing this quickly. Meanwhile, compute costs, research spending, and future capital needs make it difficult to turn that revenue into sustainable profits. Additionally, stock-based compensation adds dilution, which will be a headwind to per-share earnings growth when the company finally becomes profitable.

The crucial question isn't whether Anthropic is a great company. It's whether its growth can justify a valuation this high.

Using Anthropic competitor, ChatGPT, I modeled three scenarios for Anthropic based on its $83 IPO price. Each one estimates 2031 revenue, net margin, and a price-to-earnings (P/E) multiple, then divides the resulting equity value by a share count that grows 2% a year. Here are my assumptions:

Here's what those assumptions imply for Anthropic's share price:

My base case assumes Anthropic grows its revenue to $400 billion by 2031 with an 18% net margin and a 25x earnings multiple. That would be a remarkable business, yet it would only lift the stock to $91, about 10% above the IPO price, or roughly 2% annually. Meanwhile, my bear case assumes slower growth, thinner margins, and a lower multiple, which would send the shares down roughly 73%. It would take my bull case, with a 24% net margin and a 30x earnings multiple, to deliver a strong return of about 19% annually.

The base case return isn't compelling. Anthropic could execute extremely well and still deliver underwhelming returns because the IPO price already reflects so much success. That's the key takeaway: explosive business growth doesn't guarantee an attractive investment return.

Extending the base case to 2036 improves the outlook, but not by much. Here are the assumptions:

Those assumptions are demanding, as Anthropic would need to double its 2031 revenue and keep widening its margins. Yet the payoff would be only about an 8% annualized return.

I want to earn at least 10% annually from a stock like this using conservative assumptions. For Anthropic's base-case price of $91 in 2031 to deliver that return, I'd need to buy at around $56 per share. That's roughly 30% below the IPO price.

That's why I'd rather wait for a better entry point than chase an elevated first-day price. I'd prefer to buy closer to $55-$60 per share. Even then, I'd want to see that Anthropic can scale its revenue, produce sustainable margins, and defend its position against rival AI platforms.

Anthropic could become one of the most important technology companies of the next decade, and its revenue growth and enterprise adoption are extraordinary. However, the IPO price already reflects that potential, and my base case points to modest returns over the next five years.

I'd rather risk missing the initial excitement than overpay for an exceptional business. The stock gets much more interesting to me at $55 to $60 per share, as long as the underlying growth outlook remains intact.

Six days, six outages: Why reliability isn’t resilience

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

GUEST COLUMN by Zeus Kerravala

A rash of major outages over the past week all seem to follow a similar script. Services go dark, Downdetector lights up, the provider posts a string of status updates, a root cause emerges a few days later, and everyone goes back to work, assuming it won’t happen again. This past week, that script was on overdrive.

None of these was a cyberattack, and as far as anyone can tell, none was related. A dormant bug, overloaded legacy routers, a bad internal update and an on-premises hardware glitch are about as different as root causes get. Some of these systems run in the public cloud, some in a company’s own data center, and some on air traffic control gear that’s decades old. That’s precisely the point. The common thread isn’t the technology. It’s that each organization had a critical dependency fail, and the business stopped with it.

That’s the lesson I want business leaders to take away. Outages aren’t rare events to be explained away after the fact. They’re a certainty to plan for, and the plan must address what happens when an entire service fails, not just a server or a data center.

I wanted to dig into what real resilience looks like, and Zscaler Inc. is an interesting case study for a specific reason. When a company deploys Zscaler, every user, branch and device connects to applications through its Zero Trust Exchange. That places Zscaler squarely in the critical path. If it goes down, users can’t reach Microsoft 365, Salesforce, internal apps or anything else, no matter how healthy those applications are. That all-or-nothing exposure has forced Zscaler to think harder than most about what happens when its service fails.

At the last Zenith Live, Zscaler’s user event, I asked Chief Executive Jay Chaudhry about this, and he explained that because of the critical nature of their offering, it can’t afford any downtime, so the company built resiliency into its service design. After all these outages, I asked to speak with Chaudhry and Chief Reliability Officer Misha Kuperman to gather lessons learned for the industry.

To be clear, Zscaler couldn’t have kept a single Microsoft mailbox online or restarted the FAA’s routers, and no one at the company claimed otherwise. But its approach to continuity for its own platform is a useful model for how any service on a company’s critical path, including software-as-a-service, should be architected. Here are my thoughts on what this means for boards and C-suite leaders:

I came from financial services, where the disaster recovery site was non-negotiable and everyone knew which services had to survive a failure. Chaudhry, who spent years at IBM Corp., made the same point.

“Most of my large customers had a production data center and a DR data center,” he told me. “If the production data center went down, they’d fail over to DR. That was standard practice. When the world moved to the cloud, they forgot about it, thinking disaster recovery was the cloud provider’s responsibility, even though it is actually a shared responsibility.”

He’s right, and the record proves it. Availability zones and multiregion designs protect against hardware failures and localized events, but they share software, control planes, configuration pipelines and often DNS. When a bad configuration or a dormant bug propagates, it spreads everywhere. That’s exactly what happened to Microsoft.

Availability architecture and resilience architecture aren’t the same thing. One keeps a service up when a component fails. The other keeps the business running when the entire service fails. Most companies have the first and assume they have the second, but as we have seen recently, that assumption is incorrect.

During the call, Chaudhry used an analogy that everyone can understand. You can own the world’s most reliable car and service it religiously, yet you still buy insurance for other risks not covered by the warranty, such as theft or a tree falling on it. Having insurance and knowing when and how to use it helps you get back on the road quickly. Zscaler has built the cyber equivalent.

In the real world, people understand it. In cyber and digital, we seem to forget and rely on service-level agreements. A 99.999% uptime commitment sounds good until you translate it into business terms. Chaudhry said some of Zscaler’s largest banking customers told the company that five nines wasn’t enough, “because we could lose hundreds of millions of dollars if you went down even for five minutes.”

Going back to my IT days, I’ve never liked SLAs because they are financial instruments, not operational ones. They tell you what credit you’ll receive after the fact, not how you’ll keep processing payments, as Chase customers learned, or how you’ll keep planes in the air, as United learned. Regulators have recognized this. The EU’s Digital Operational Resilience Act and Australia’s CPS 230 both require regulated firms to assess, test, and mitigate the risk posed by their technology providers. “Our cloud provider went down” is no longer an acceptable defense.

IT executives should stop asking, “What’s our provider’s SLA?” and start asking, “What must our business continue to do when a critical dependency fails, and have we proven we can do so?”

The instinctive response to an outage like Microsoft’s is to buy from a second provider. Chaudhry shared a story about a chief information officer whose CEO had flagged Office 365 as a business risk and told him to find an alternative. The CIO signed up for Google Workspace. When asked whether documents, links and role-based access controls would work after a switch, even in half the cases, he said he didn’t think so.

That’s the reality. Email alone is hard enough because users lose their history the moment they switch. Modern productivity suites aren’t storage anymore. Every email includes links to shared documents, permissions are tied to identity, and workflows span multiple apps. Kuperman framed the problem in terms of scale: “I can switch between Gmail and Outlook, but can 10,000 people? Will they be as productive?”

The same problem applies in security and networking, where the stakes are arguably higher. The vendor A/vendor B model requires two policy sets kept in perfect sync, two client software sets and a way to move tens of thousands of users and devices from one to the other in the middle of a crisis. Kuperman described one customer for whom it “used to take seven days to switch over and 30 days to switch back. You can’t run a business like that.”

A backup that’s never tested at full scale isn’t a backup. It’s a hope. And a backup that changes the user experience will fail exactly when it’s needed.

Another instinct is to turn off security entirely; however, when a crisis occurs, threat actors will rally and launch campaigns. During the CrowdStrike outage, phishing campaigns were mobilized almost immediately, and the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency had to issue warnings not to take the bait. Threat actors are present both internally and externally, so security must remain on guard during critical failures, especially when they are publicly known.

This brings me to what Zscaler has done with its Business Continuity Cloud and why I think it’s the right template for the industry. Rather than asking customers to bolt on a second vendor, Zscaler built a dedicated, per-customer environment that’s architecturally separate from its primary Zero Trust Exchange.

The design choices are important to understand. The Business Continuity Cloud runs in third-party data centers, not Zscaler’s own, on separate networks, autonomous system numbers, DNS, and cloud domains. Software and threat-intelligence updates are deliberately staggered so a bad production release can’t land in the backup at the same time. Policy and identity data sync one way into a read-only, last-known-good state. It’s offered across nine regions, enough to keep operations in-country if a nation were cut off.

“No linkage — no network, no shared routers, no switches, no shared network operations,” Chaudhry said. That’s the old production/DR discipline, rebuilt for the cloud. After watching overloaded routers ground the nation’s air traffic, “no shared routers” doesn’t sound like overkill.

There were two operational details I felt were worth noting. First, Zscaler encourages customers to continuously route synthetic or trusted-user traffic through the backup environment in peacetime, so the failover path is validated daily rather than in an annual tabletop exercise. Second, the failover is automated to optimize for a low recovery time objective or RTO, or it can be triggered by the customer, ensuring it works even if Zscaler itself is unreachable.

Zscaler says nearly 100 customers now use its service, including a global bank with more than 300,000 users, a global pharmaceutical company with more than 100,000, and a U.S. telco with more than 75,000. The company charges what Chaudhry called a nominal fee to cover the dedicated infrastructure, network and always-on capacity.

The lesson from the Microsoft outage, in particular, isn’t that customers need a second productivity suite. It’s that the primary provider should offer a fate-separated continuity environment as part of the service. Zscaler has shown it can be done for a global security cloud. I’d like to see every SaaS provider that sits in a company’s critical path, and Microsoft in particular, be pressed by large customers to offer the same.

The Microsoft outage also exposed a concentration risk that many companies have faced through enterprise licensing agreements. Microsoft said the incident affected not only Exchange and Teams but also Defender XDR, Defender for Office 365 and Intune. For many organizations, Microsoft is the productivity suite, the identity provider and the security stack. When the shared infrastructure beneath them fails, all three can fail together.

Kuperman described the minimum set of capabilities a business needs to keep operating during a disaster: people, identity, network and security, and business-critical applications. Each must work, and any one can fail. “You have to think about where your eggs are and which baskets they’re in,” he said.

I’m not arguing against platforms. But boards should understand where consolidation has created a single point of failure across identity, applications and security, and ensure those layers don’t share the same fate.

This won’t be the last major outage, and the next one may be a cyberattack, a war, or a configuration push rather than a dormant bug. Here’s what I recommend IT and business leaders do now:

The cloud delivered on its promise of agility and scale, but in the process, the industry let a hard-won discipline lapse. This past week reminded us that everything fails eventually, whether in the cloud, a data center or an air traffic control tower. When it does, an SLA credit won’t keep the business running. A real continuity plan will.

Zeus Kerravala is a principal analyst at ZK Research, a division of Kerravala Consulting. He wrote this article for SiliconANGLE.

I Had To Know Why Rings Of Power’s Dark Wizard Ended Up Really Being The (Spoiler) Wizard

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The New LOTR Is Heavily Under Wraps, But Jamie Dornan Just Made Two Big Admissions

Why Stepping Back Into His Hobbit Feet For LOTR’s Hunt For Gollum Was ‘Surprising’ For Elijah Wood

There’s A New Harry Potter TV Clip, And Now I’m Even More Excited For John Lithgow As Dumbledore

Spoilers for the first five episodes of Rings of Power Season 3 are ahead! You can stream the series with an Amazon Prime subscription.

On Rings of Power, we’ve spent seasons trying to figure out the identities of the wizards who were sent to Middle-earth. In Season 2, after we spent a lot of time guessing and theorizing, we learned that The Stranger was indeed Gandalf. However, the identity of the Dark Wizard was still unknown. In Season 3, his true name was finally revealed, though, and I asked the co-creator of the show why they wanted to make the Dark Wizard the Blue Wizard Pallando.

In Episode 3 of the season of Rings of Power that’s currently airing on the 2026 TV schedule, Gandalf has an interaction with the Secret Fire, where it’s revealed to him that five wizards were sent to save Middle-earth. That’s how we learned that Pallando, one of the two Blue Wizards, killed his partner, and it’s also when Gandalf learns that he’s been sent to save the corrupted Blue Wizard. When I asked co-creator Patrick McKay why they wanted to make the Dark Wizard Pallando, he began by saying:

Here's the thing that we do on the show: We trust our audience. We're really thinking of it as one 50-hour movie. That's always been the plan and the aspiration. That also means that when we introduce a new character, you might not know where his or her allegiances lie. You might not know his or her name, but we know, and we have a plan.

While the end of Rings of Power Season 2 made me think the Dark Wizard was Saruman (even though that actually makes no sense timeline-wise), the other prevailing theory was that he was a Blue Wizard. Now, it’s confirmed that that’s the case, and McKay told me they made this decision because of how fascinating the lore is behind these two rather mysterious characters:

And you know there is this great lore in Tolkien about the two blue wizards who went east. And Tolkien had different versions of it and different ideas he played with. And a recurrent theme was that maybe one of them went bad, and no one knows what happened to the other one. And then, you know, thinking about that and bringing our wizard, our hero wizard Gandalf, sounds good to say that now, into that story, now you're weaving, right?

At one point, Tolkien wrote that the Blue Wizards could have been “founders or beginners of secret cults and magic traditions that outlasted the fall of Sauron” (via ScreenRant). It’s not confirmed if this is used as inspiration for Rings of Power; however, it does point to the potential moral ambiguity surrounding these magical beings.

On the show, they ultimately decided to run with the notion that Pallando might have done something terrible and killed his partner. Exploring the complexities of that was very interesting to them, as McKay told me:

What might be interesting? Well, gosh, what if Pallando has murdered his partner, and that's where he got his power from, but maybe there's complexity to it. Maybe he has guilt over it. Maybe, you know, he even has a desire for redemption. But how much? And then building a story around that was one of the joys of this season. But you know, the idea of the Blue Wizards is always tantalizing to us as fans, and part of our job is the inner fanboy getting to play.

Well, now I’m certainly excited to see where things go from here. I want to see Gandalf and Pallando interact more. I want to know if Gandalf can help his fellow wizard get away from the evil. And I want to know more about why the Blue Wizard killed his friend.

Hopefully, that will be unveiled as Rings of Power wraps up its third season and releases Episodes 7 and 8 on November 25.

Did a Narcissistic Boss Cost You Your Career?

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 18, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

Prada was a 29-year-old registered nurse when she came to my office in tears, saying she hated her job and was having panic attacks. Sharon, the hospital’s human resources director, perceived that the trauma exposure in her work in the emergency room was the source of the panic attacks and sent her to specialized resilience training.

Devin, a 42-year-old software architect, came to my office with concerns about depression. He feared losing his job because he was too old and believed that new artificial intelligence programs were replacing him.

At first glance, their struggles seemed understandable, given the unique demands of their professions. The trauma exposure, aging concerns, and technological pressures were real. But more conversations revealed something deeper and more destabilizing: a high-achieving, abusive, and narcissistic boss.

The challenge is that organizations often view a high-achiever as the goose that lays the golden egg, making them revered and supported. But when that high-achiever also commits narcissistic abuse, they can be untouchable. And the fallout of their abusive tactics resides in the long-term wounds of countless victims.

In It's Not You: Identifying and Healing from Narcissistic People, Ramani Durvasula describes how many therapists are trained to address the person’s isolated symptoms and often miss the larger impacts of narcissistic relationships. She also echoes what Lundy Bancroft explained almost 25 years ago in Why Does He Do That? Inside the Minds of Angry and Controlling Men—the abusive person is a master of disguise and actually gains footing with the therapist, while the abused person who shows up as anxious and closed can get further gaslit and retraumatized by the therapist. Moreover, interventions focused on appeasement or better communication can reinforce and escalate manipulation and abusive tactics.

Now imagine that third party is HR, an executive coach, or an organizational consultant.

And imagine that the abusive person is exceptionally accomplished, charismatic, influential, and valuable to the organization.

Who gets believed? Who gets coached? Who gets labeled difficult? And who quietly disappears from the organization?

Given these complex dynamics, I was surprised to read an article in the Journal of Business Ethics suggesting that clear codes of conduct and consequences could help address narcissistically abusive supervision. The authors’ research revealed that leaders high in narcissistic rivalry employ abusive supervision when they perceive employees as deviating from their authority or organizational rules; yet they suggest: “It is essential that organizations recognize the importance of fostering a supportive environment in which both leaders and followers are equipped with the skills and knowledge to interact respectfully with each other” (Gauglitz & Schyns, 2024).

While rules for conduct can establish best practices and delineate what is prohibited, they may fail to address the hidden dynamics of manipulation, coercion, and stealth sabotage.

In Ten Keys to Staying Empowered in a Power Struggle, I explain how manipulative tactics often operate through confusing double-binds, intermittent praise, shifting expectations, selective memory, and gaslighting. These patterns can leave people questioning their judgment while the person causing harm appears credible and composed.

I have witnessed people who left jobs—and entire careers—without understanding how an abusive workplace dynamic eroded their confidence, health, and sense of agency.

If something like this happened to you, or someone you know, consider these five questions:

In contrast, healthy workplace dynamics and leadership allow disagreement without humiliation, accountability without retaliation, and growth without diminishing someone's dignity.

Sometimes, recognizing what happened is the first step toward healing, which involves reclaiming what you lost, honoring what you endured, and breaking the cycle of abuse that continues long after the abuser is gone through self-blame, shame, and the transformation of someone else’s cruelty into your own inner voice.

Gauglitz, I. K., & Schyns, B. (20240801). Triggered Abuse: How and Why Leaders with Narcissistic Rivalry React to Follower Deviance. Journal of Business Ethics, 193(1), 115–131. https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-023-05579-7
