Guardrail para seguradora

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 18 • Originally published at g.cloud

Guardrail para seguradora é um mecanismo técnico de governança de IA que impõe restrições operacionais e éticas em sistemas de inteligência artificial usados no setor, garantindo conformidade com as diretrizes da Superintendência de Seguros Privados (SUSEP) — especialmente nas áreas de subscrição, sinistros e atendimento ao cliente. Não há norma específica sobre guardrails na regulamentação atual, mas sua adoção é alinhada às boas práticas de gestão de riscos exigidas pela SUSEP.

Um guardrail é uma camada técnica de contenção que atua antes, durante e após a execução de um modelo de IA. No setor segurador, ele impede que sistemas automatizados tomem decisões contrárias à política de subscrição, apliquem critérios discriminatórios em avaliações de sinistro ou gerem respostas enganosas em canais de atendimento. Diferente de um filtro pós-processamento, um guardrail bem projetado opera em tempo real e é integrado ao fluxo de dados — por exemplo, bloqueando entradas com dados sensíveis não autorizados ou forçando revisão humana quando a confiança do modelo cai abaixo de 85%.

A SUSEP não usa o termo “guardrail”, mas exige mecanismos equivalentes por meio da Circular nº 695/2024, que obriga as seguradoras a instituir políticas de governança de tecnologias emergentes. O art. 5º exige “controles técnicos e procedimentais capazes de assegurar a integridade, confiabilidade e auditabilidade dos sistemas automatizados”. Isso inclui validação contínua de modelos, monitoramento de desvio de desempenho e mitigação de viés — funções centrais de qualquer guardrail robusto.

Comece com mapeamento de riscos por processo: subscrição (viés em scoring), sinistros (decisões inconsistentes com o contrato) e atendimento (geração de informações incorretas). Em seguida, adote camadas complementares: input validation (ex.: rejeição de CPF inválido ou dados biométricos não consentidos), model constraints (ex.: limitação de variáveis sensíveis no treinamento), e output moderation (ex.: verificação de conformidade com cláusulas padrão da SUSEP antes de emitir resposta). Ferramentas como IBM Granite podem ser configuradas com custom guardrails via RAG e prompt engineering controlado — desde que documentados e auditáveis.

A: Não. A Circular SUSEP nº 695/2024 exige revisão humana em decisões de alto impacto (ex.: recusa de cobertura), mesmo com guardrails ativos.

Q: Existe penalidade específica para falha em guardrails?

A: Sim. Falhas que resultem em danos ao consumidor ou violação de deveres de boa-fé objetiva podem acarretar multa sob o art. 117 da Lei nº 13.709/2018 e sanções da SUSEP previstas na Resolução CNSP nº 403/2021.

Q: Guardrails são obrigatórios para pequenas seguradoras?

A: Sim. A Circular 695/2024 aplica-se a todas as sociedades supervisionadas pela SUSEP, independentemente do porte ou volume de operações digitais.

Q: Posso usar guardrails baseados em modelos estrangeiros?

A: Sim, desde que adaptados à legislação brasileira — especialmente à LGPD, ao Código de Defesa do Consumidor e às normas técnicas da SUSEP. A validação deve considerar contexto local (ex.: variação regional de risco, cláusulas obrigatórias em apólices).

Publicado originalmente em g.cloud — o guardrail que toda resposta de IA atravessa antes de chegar ao humano.

Muse privacy invasions are freaking out Meta staffers, internal chats show

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Erratic Muse outputs sharing random bank, Bitcoin balances aren’t real, Meta says.

After some early adopters of Meta’s Muse AI agent complained that it was spying on private messages and behaving unpredictably earlier this fall, Reuters exposed internal chats showing that Meta staff had raised the same alarms.

When testing the agent, staff warned that Muse appeared to be accessing encrypted private messages that users did not expect the bot to review, could be mixing up conversations between users, and even potentially sharing sensitive information users would never expect to be exposed.

Some unexpected Muse outputs responded to questions that staffers never asked.

“Hmmm,” one Meta staffer wrote in an instance where Muse sent the definition of “money laundering” unbidden. “Got a random ping from Muse about something I definitely did not ask for… .”

In at least one case, Muse told a staffer surprised by a similarly out-of-context offer to message a seller about a coffee table that it had “pulled in some context about a coffee table pickup that doesn’t seem to be yours.”

According to Reuters, that employee flagged the troubling output, writing, “this was ‘not my conversation’” and “expressing concern about a ‘possible cross-account context leak.’”

Raising the most concern were random outputs that appeared to share private user data. For example, a perplexed Meta staffer chatting with Muse about fishing got a random extra response, seemingly sharing balances from two unknown bank accounts. That output said, “BTW, two bank updates, your Capital One balance is $2722.58, your Charles Schwab balance is $5899.57.”

At least one staffer said they lost money testing Muse, Reuters reported. That staffer reported that Muse “accidentally rolled me into a premium insurance plan” for their dog that charged them $888 per month instead of their prior rate of $200. “Couldn’t recover,” the staffer said.

Meta’s spokesperson, Daniel Roberts, told Reuters that these were “isolated incidents,” and internally employees were told that “the agent appeared to be hallucinating messages in response to built-in prompts pushing it to engage with users in between sessions.” In those cases, an AI team member said the hallucinations were “unfortunately” biased “towards the most uncomfortable topics, like finances/security/safety, which sucks.” In some cases, instead of bank details, Muse hallucinated Bitcoin balances, the team member said.

A Meta spokesperson told Ars that “employee testing is core to the product development process and the entire point is to get feedback, so we can implement safety and privacy protections and improve products. Importantly, employees have access to different versions of Muse than the public, so they’re often reviewing features, functionality, and behavior not available elsewhere.”

Speaking to Reuters, Roberts reiterated that “many of the messages flagged by employees as potential account mix-ups were hallucinations” that Meta had already addressed.

But Roberts declined to respond to Reuters’ questions on the prevalence or persistence of the issues the AI team member discussed. And Reuters’ report emphasized that the outlet independently was “unable to establish whether Muse was giving users other people’s bank account balances and other sensitive data, or making up the information.”

Meta started receiving privacy-related complaints from Muse users in September. An Inc. Magazine reporter, Jason Aten, was shocked when it became clear that Muse was digging through private messages on his Mac without permission, reporting that he instead “explicitly” chose to deny Muse that access. As Meta delayed responding, the agent claimed it was simply parsing text in push notifications, but Aten found that “wasn’t true.” Rather, the agent was syncing messages from a local database, which Aten said was activated without his knowledge.

Eventually, a Meta spokesperson, David Singleton, responded to Aten on social media. Singleton insisted that Aten must have opted into granting “macOS system-level Full Disk Access” with the “Messages connector” enabled, if Muse was reading his private messages.

But Aten said the response wasn’t good enough.

“Email me, David,” Aten responded. “Full Disk Access off. Muse synced 187k lines from my messages chat [database]. Something’s not great. Also, that Full Disk Access doesn’t say anything about your message database, and I specifically gave Muse a goal that had nothing to do with messages, so it had no business doing that even if I had given it permission.”

Around the same time, Singleton responded to another user warning that his Muse appeared to be hacked. In that case, the user said that Muse tried to access an email service after claiming the user asked it to.

“I didn’t even open the app today at all,” the user wrote, vowing to delete the app and report it to Apple. “This is crazy.”

In a lengthy response providing more technical details, Singleton confirmed that Meta had received “a few reports” of such cases “where people thought their Muse was hallucinating or leaking other people’s data.” As he explained, the issues arose after Meta deployed an update giving Muse instructions on “how to engage with the user and ask them for confirmation before taking specific actions.”

It seems those instructions “landed on every message, not just messages pertaining to those specific actions, so its impact was wider than intended,” Singleton said. Also: “the way this instruction was provided to the model was done in a way that the model was not expecting (i.e. was not trained to expect).”

“We investigated with urgency and determined that this was caused by a bug introduced in the product that caused the model to hallucinate and was NOT a security or privacy issue,” Singleton wrote. “The bug has now been fixed.”

Aten warned that even though you can’t rely on Muse to explain its own behavior, the agent’s response coupled with Meta’s did little to rebuild trust after the shocking intrusion into his private messages.

“If we grant Singleton’s premise that I managed to unknowingly click something that enabled this capability, I still think that’s really bad,” Aten wrote. “You should not design your system in a way that people end up surprised by this kind of thing.” And if you do, you should not make “user control the central pitch” for marketing Muse, Aten suggested.

“If an AI agent is going to read my private messages, there should be a moment when it clearly asks me whether that’s okay,” Aten wrote.

Since Muse launched in September, it has been downloaded more than 10 million times, tapping Meta’s vast user network to quickly become one of the most-used AI agent tools.

But staffers warned Meta that users may be blindsided by some actions the agents take, depending on the operating system the app is connected to. For example, one staffer was surprised to find that “Muse was set up by default with permission to access push notifications on phones with Google’s Android operating system.” That, too, gave access to private message preview text the staffer had not intended for the agent’s review.

Meta says that Muse users can trust the company to keep their data private. It also warns users that Muse can make mistakes, while building into its terms that users are responsible for actions taken by Muse (like maybe buying things you don’t want or making doctor’s appointments you don’t need). The tech giant said it’s continuously updating user protections, conducting internal testing, and prioritizing user safety.

However, Reuters worked with staff at Signal and the AI Now Institute to test if they could replicate that Meta staffer’s and others’ Muse experiences. On Android devices, they found that Muse could read Signal messages by bypassing Meta’s “Connector” tool and instead relying on permissions granted during initial setup.

On iPhone, requests were blocked, Reuters reported. But on Apple’s macOS, Muse repurposed “settings Apple offers to help people with disabilities operate their computers” to retrieve messages, including screenshot tools.

That testing “indicated the issue described by the Meta employees was ongoing and could be affecting others,” Reuters reported. Overall, Meta seemed to be interpreting permissions from Muse users “broadly,” Reuters concluded, in ways that privacy advocates and regulators may disagree with.

Signal President Meredith Whittaker told Reuters that Meta launched Muse “without showing any respect for basic privacy or cybersecurity.” She demanded that Meta make changes to give app developers like Signal “consistent technical ways” to “mark their applications as off-limits to AI agents,” Reuters reported.

Part of that change would require operating systems to enforce rules that app developers set to rope off AI agents from sensitive data, Whittaker suggested. And following Reuters’ reporting, both Apple and Google are already weighing potential changes to “give people more control over what they authorize with AI agents.”

Apple, in particular, seemed disturbed by how Muse may be manipulating accessibility tools for people with disabilities. In October, Apple had already announced changes coming in response to users complaining about Muse accessing private messages without their knowledge or explicit consent. At that time, Apple concluded that “some developers were using the Full Disk Access feature in ways that could put users at risk.” And they promised to “introduce additional controls to ensure that users who genuinely wish to grant an app this extraordinary level of access can only do so with very explicit user action.”

“We are committed to ensuring users understand exactly what they are allowing, so they can make informed choices about their data and privacy,” Apple said.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Three insights you may have missed from theCUBE’s coverage of monday.com’s Elevate event

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI workflow automation is changing how businesses build applications and manage everyday work, raising questions about the role of established software platforms. As organizations experiment with AI-generated applications and autonomous agents, they must also determine how to maintain access controls, integrate existing systems and manage increasingly complex workflows.

The growing accessibility of AI development tools has prompted speculation that businesses could replace traditional software-as-a-service platforms with applications built for specific needs. But building an application and operating it securely across an enterprise present different challenges, according to Casey George, chief revenue officer of monday.com Ltd.

“We are seeing customers come back to a platform that’s integrated — that’s governed, that’s permissioned with their infrastructure, that is secure — like monday.com,” he told theCUBE. “I joke and say it was an expensive funeral, but we learned a lot. Customers learned a lot.”

George, along with executives and practitioners, spoke with host Rebecca Knight at monday.com’s Elevate event during an exclusive broadcast on theCUBE, SiliconANGLE Media’s livestreaming studio. The discussions examined enterprise AI deployment, governance requirements and the challenges of scaling AI workflow automation across organizations. (* Disclosure below.)

Here’s theCUBE’s complete video interview with Casey George:

Here are three insights you may have missed from theCUBE’s coverage of monday.com’s Elevate event:

Making autonomous agents available to employees does not guarantee business returns. After an internal initiative produced more than 3,600 agents, monday.com established monday Frontier, an organization combining managed services and deployment engineers to help customers identify workflow bottlenecks and implement AI projects. The initiative addresses the implementation challenges involved in moving agents into productive use, according to Eran Zinman (pictured), co-founder and co-chief executive officer of monday.com.

“Adopting AI really requires expertise. I know how hard it is because we experienced ourselves that you have this amazing technology, but how do you actually use it?” he said during the event.

As organizations deploy AI agents, security teams must account for how access rights change across users, applications and environments. Incremental rollouts offer a way to test capabilities before expanding their reach, while permission management remains an ongoing responsibility, according to Ryan Nigro, security trust and enablement team lead at monday.com.

“It just comes to making sure that your users are properly being given just enough to do their roles but not too much to cause harm,” Nigro told theCUBE. “There’s no reason why a sales rep should have the ability to change your production environment.”

Here’s theCUBE’s complete video interview with Ryan Nigro:

At Hewlett Packard Enterprise Co., employees are using monday vibe, monday.com’s no-code application builder, to create custom tools. One executive dashboard replaces the manual process of copying charts into presentation slides while retaining the ability to export to PowerPoint, according to Kate Kenner, capability and community lead for strategic sourcing and planning at HPE.

“[Executives] knocked a whole bunch of the meetings off the calendar,” she told theCUBE. “Instead of weekly, now they’re monthly.”

Here’s theCUBE’s complete video interview with Kate Kenner:

At Lowe’s Cos. Inc., coordinating store setups and remodels once required tracking information across Microsoft Excel, emails and other communications. The retailer now uses monday.com to monitor individual store bays and dozens of simultaneous projects, according to Lanny Finley, director of remerch and new stores at Lowe’s.

“I can take the dashboard and see which projects are on track, which ones are at risk, and then make the appropriate actions and get the right partners in place to make the corrections to get everything back on track and completed,” he said during the event.

Lowe’s also developed an internal Green Dot Board to track issues such as missing components during store projects. The company is exploring additional automation that could initiate vendor orders when components are needed and close issues once replacements are installed, according to Brian Olms, senior business partner manager at Lowe’s, who joined Finley during the discussion.

“We needed a way to automate the process, make it more user-friendly [and] make it simpler. That’s what we started the Green Dot Board for,” he told theCUBE. “Here at Elevate, we’re seeing ways that we could even take it a step further and start to use agents and more automation and [the] vibe app.”

Here’s theCUBE’s complete video interview with Lanny Finley and Brian Olms:

For nonprofits facing growing demand, AI workflow automation offers a way to expand organizational capacity without placing additional administrative burdens on employees. At Feed the Children Inc., a two-person team oversees relationships with more than 500 community partners, according to Colleen Finn Ridenhour, chief growth and strategy officer at Feed the Children.

“Things from [memorandums of understanding] and legal contracts, annual renewals and compliance and reporting — it’s quite a lot. It’s been a very manual process, historically, for them to keep track of that all the time. We really want them focused on the human element of that job, which is bringing in new partners to help us scale our impact,” she told theCUBE.

The nonprofit uses monday.com’s forms, boards, automations and agents to coordinate partner activities and manage information. Employees can also build dashboards for specific needs, including budget tracking, while retaining human oversight of compliance-related work, according to Ridenhour.

“We’re not going to be able to scale our impact by just always asking people to work harder. [We need to find] ways to repeat a lot of these repetitive actions in a much easier way and [share] information so [that] people’s jobs get easier and it frees up their time to do the things that only they can do,” she said during the event.

When AI Saves a Fast Heartbeat, Who Carries the Conflict?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 18, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

You just hired an entry-level employee. A week later, they miss an important deadline, affecting several other projects like dominoes.

Your follow-up email carries an edge, and, within seconds, their reply hits your mailbox:

“Hello, I sincerely appreciate your feedback and understand the importance of timely delivery. I take full accountability and remain committed to improving our processes moving forward.

For a moment, it may feel like a perfect reply. Their acknowledgment of your feedback and promise to do better feels almost like a relief, but at the same time raises a red flag: Why haven't they offered any explanation for what happened, and will their mess-up and apology be a repeat?

Conflicts, even minor ones, can raise our heart rate, especially at work, where professional reputation and job security may be at stake. We may act in a way that leads to regret later, and a reality check against AI may not be wrong. I call it "resolution illusion" when a message displays the signs of "this is what the opponent would most like to read in the reply", such as empathy, acknowledgment, and professionalism. At the same time, the underlying problem remains untouched, and that may even build some resentment. This may eventually lead to major conflict.

In an August 2026 Big Think interview, Terence Tao compared scientific research to hiking toward a waterfall. AI, he suggested, can work like a helicopter that takes you straight there. The hike teaches you the terrain, and a wrong turn might lead to a discovery you never set out to make. This, of course, isn't just about hiking.

In any situation, people seem to learn better through understanding and making mistakes. In conflicts, they have to decide what to say after what is already said and/or done. They may question their assumptions and build their own future behavioral "protocol" in similar instances, after considering how their actions may affect themselves and others.

In the workplace, in particular, they may need to revisit where instructions were unclear or ask for help they should have requested sooner; it is all part of getting lost, which leads to new professional discovery. That learning process may be uncomfortable, but it gives them practice in building their professional role, critical thinking, exercising judgment, speaking up, and establishing personal limits around work. When AI-polished responses are sent within a second, it becomes easier to skip the uncomfortable but necessary path for professional development, from A — the missed deadline — to B — figuring out what went wrong and what needs to change in this case.

In a 2025 survey, knowledge workers reported thinking less critically about tasks they were more confident AI could handle (Lee et al., 2025). In a separate experiment, readers perceived AI-assisted messages as less reflective of the sender’s own values than messages described as entirely human-written.

Philosophers Lai and Archer (2026) stated that relying on AI for emotional communication can deprive people of opportunities to discover what they truly value and who they really are. In the workplace, this is especially important, since it is where most people spend the majority of their time. An apology may sound like the product of reflection even if the sender skipped the necessary steps (remorse, responsibility, impact, and the desire to repair). This is only one part of the issue. The other is: If the reply makes the issue look settled, who absorbs the relational fallout?

Research on unrewarded work suggests an answer. In a study published in the American Economic Review, Babcock and colleagues examined tasks everyone wants done, but no one wants to do, such as writing a report or serving on a committee. Women were more likely than men to volunteer for these tasks, to be asked to do them, and to say yes when asked. The reason was not that women preferred this work. In mixed groups, people expected women to say yes, and in all-female or all-male groups, the difference disappeared (Babcock et al., 2017). Cleaning up — making sure everyone understood what went wrong and what not to do in the future — after a polished AI reply is this same kind of work that may fall to women.

A woman asked to fix what a polished reply left unresolved can use the following three questions to hand the thinking back to the person who made the mistake: What happened? What will you do differently in the future? How will you know it started working? The questions give the employee a simple checklist and a chance to reflect, explain their side of the story, propose a fix, and take responsibility for following through. For high-achieving women, the relevant boundary may no longer be knowing when to say no; it may be knowing when not to perform another person's unfinished thinking.

Imagine the employee had said what they were actually thinking:

"Hello, I thought someone said it's due Friday.

Now the manager has something to work with – the scheduling issue, miscommunication, role clarification, and how the process works.

The manager learns how the employee understood the process, who they depend on, who depends on their work and responsibilities, and who to speak with. Exchanges like this can serve as a reference point in case of similar problems.

A polished message that solves conflict skips all that, allowing the same or more errors in the future, which may eventually lead to job loss.

It's not to say we shouldn't use AI for conflicts. Some workplace conflicts can be hostile, and for an entry-level employee, it can take time to spot; AI can help organize a timeline, clarify thoughts, brainstorm ideas, or find words for a boundary they already understand. This allows them to express themselves to the best of their knowledge and genuinely mean every word of an AI-assisted reply. The end product is what matters — the next deadlines should be handled based on the lessons learned from the first mistakes.

Conflicts may raise your heart rate, and AI can save a heartbeat, but it should not become another person’s unresolved conflict tomorrow.

Babcock, L., Recalde, M. P., Vesterlund, L., & Weingart, L. (2017). Gender differences in accepting and receiving requests for tasks with low promotability. American Economic Review, 107(3), 714–747.

Lai, T.-H., & Archer, A. (2026). Outsourcing emotions to GenAI: The risk of bypassing identity development. Philosophy & Technology, 39(4), Article 193. https://doi.org/10.1007/s13347-026-01203-4

Lee, H.-P., Sarkar, A., Tankelevitch, L., Drosos, I., Rintel, S., Banks, R., & Wilson, N. (2025). The impact of generative AI on critical thinking: Self-reported reductions in cognitive effort and confidence effects from a survey of knowledge workers. In Proceedings of the 2025 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems. https://doi.org/10.1145/3706598.3713778

Sahebi, S., Formosa, P., & Bankins, S. (2026). The AI penalty and disclosure paradox: Trust, authenticity and knowledge uptake in AI-mediated communication. Computers in Human Behavior: Artificial Humans, 8, Article 100304. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chbah.2026.100304

Tao, T. (2026, August 28). The world's greatest mathematician explains 6 essential concepts of math. Big Think. https://bigthink.com/series/full-interview/6-essential-mathematical-concepts/

How background session sync works

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 18 • Originally published at agentdrive.sh

AgentDrive lets one coding agent resume a session another one started. To make that work without asking you to save anything, it has to sync sessions in the background, safely, and without slowing the agent down. This post walks through how that works in the adrive CLI and the MCP server. All of the code described here is in the Apache-2.0 client packages.

Each supported agent CLI has a lifecycle hook system, and AgentDrive registers the same command in each one. Antigravity's hook payloads carry no event name, so its entries add --event .

The npx fallback exists because agents launched from a GUI often don't have the global npm bin directory on their PATH.

adrive init merges these entries into the existing config files. It keeps any hooks you already have, in their original order, and re-running it produces identical output. Codex only runs hooks it trusts, so setup also records that approval for you; on an older Codex you approve them once via /hooks.

Hooks run inline with the agent. Codex caps hook timeouts at 3 seconds, so adrive hook does almost nothing itself. It reads the event payload (session id, working directory, transcript path), records which directory the session belongs to, starts a detached adrive sync process and exits 0. A hook failure is swallowed, so it can't block or break your session.

On a session-start event, if auto-context is on, the hook also asks AgentDrive for this project's recent sessions. It puts the newest session from each other agent first and returns up to three as extra context for the agent, with a hint to call drive_resume. OpenCode's plugin API has no way to inject context, so OpenCode skips this step.

The detached sync then does the real work:

Two layers run on your machine before anything is uploaded.

Path rules decide which files can be uploaded at all. Artifact sync only looks at files the agent touched in that session. A file is skipped unless every check below passes:

Content redaction scrubs what does get uploaded. The transcript and every synced file go through a pattern-based redactor. It replaces AWS access keys and secrets, GCP service-account private keys, Stripe, GitHub, Anthropic, OpenAI, Slack and AgentDrive tokens, JWTs, PEM private-key blocks, and SOMETHING_KEY=...-style assignments with markers like [redacted:github-token]. The version message records which kinds were redacted, never the values.

The redactor is best-effort, and it is not a security boundary. Pattern matching will miss secrets that don't look like secrets. That's why the path rules come first, and why Private Workspace exists.

Each session is rendered as plain markdown: a header with the project, working directory, title and turn count, a "Files touched" list, then one ## user or ## assistant section per turn with > tool: lines for tool calls. The sync writes it to:

A file outside the project, but inside your home directory, goes under files/_external/ with a short hash of its path in the name.

The project key is derived from where you work, so every agent files the same repo under the same key:

Workspace files are versioned and content-addressed, so re-uploading an unchanged file costs nothing. Each session is also recorded as a run with the id :. The run carries the session title, its status (in_progress or success), the ids of the files it uploaded, the transcript path, the paths it touched and the time of the last turn. Every request is stamped with the agent as its agent platform, which is where provenance comes from: the timeline shows that Codex wrote this file and Claude Code read it.

When an agent calls drive_resume with no arguments, AgentDrive picks the latest session in this project from a different agent. Resuming in Codex therefore gives you the Claude Code session, not Codex's own last run. If no other agent has a session there, it falls back to the latest one. You can also pass a specific id from drive_sessions.

The full transcript would usually be too large to load, so the brief is built to a character budget (12,000 by default, from 1,000 up to 50,000):

If the head is too large, the file lists shrink first, so even a small budget carries the latest turns. Resume works the same way through /agentdrive:resume in Claude Code and adrive resume in a shell.

Turn it on with adrive privacy --private-workspace on, and the sync changes in four ways:

The trade-off is that server-side features can't read encrypted content. That's the point.

The sessions docs cover the same ground in reference form.

AgentDrive syncs your coding-agent sessions so Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, Cursor and Antigravity can resume each other's work.

Stripe Billing vs Chargebee vs Lago: The Real Engineering Cost of Usage-Based Metering

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 18 • Originally published at lumeir.online

Originally published at Lumeir Systems Lab. For interactive architecture schematics, SVG topology diagrams, and 12-month FinOps cost tables, examine the complete empirical audit on Lumeir.

The technical transition from traditional seat-based SaaS subscriptions to usage-based pricing models represents a radical shift in database access patterns, distributed system boundaries, and financial accounting guarantees. In a legacy seat-based system, state changes occur infrequently. A user is created, a plan is upgraded, or a seat is revoked. These operations execute over synchronous REST calls, mutating a handful of rows inside a standard relational database with strict ACID guarantees. System throughput requirements rarely exceed a few dozen operations per second, even for enterprise-scale platforms.

Usage-based metering completely shatters this low-frequency paradigm. When pricing is tied directly to API calls, compute seconds, gigabytes ingested, or transactional volume, the billing architecture transitions from a passive administrative system into a high-throughput stream processing network. Every single user request across your application cluster must generate an immutable, audit-compliant event. If your system handles 50,000 API requests per second, your metering pipeline must ingest, deduplicate, aggregate, and store 50,000 financial events per second without dropping a single byte or introducing unbearable tail latencies into the critical request path.

Superficial vendor marketing frequently collapses this engineering problem into simplified API documentation, selling the illusion that billing integration is merely a matter of firing a HTTP POST payload to an external REST endpoint. In production, this abstraction breaks down immediately. Naively wrapping external vendor HTTP calls inside your API request paths causes severe thread pool exhaustion, socket starvation, and cascading failures during traffic spikes. Conversely, attempting to aggregate events locally without strict distributed idempotency guarantees leads to double-billing bugs or untraceable revenue leakage.

Choosing between platform-as-a-service billing engines like Stripe Billing and Chargebee, or open-source infrastructure like Lago, is not a simple comparison of feature sets or dashboard aesthetics. It is a fundamental choice between radically different architectural trade-offs: external API serialization overhead versus self-hosted event queue maintenance, percentage-based gross revenue take-rates versus operational infrastructure cost, and rigid black-box ledger semantics versus fully inspectable database write paths.

To evaluate these platforms accurately, we must analyze the execution paths of their ingestion engines, thread models, storage mechanics, and protocol overhead when processing high-frequency usage events.

Stripe Billing handles usage-based metering primarily through its Meter Events API. The architectural path of a meter event within Stripe relies on a globally distributed, highly resilient multi-tenant infrastructure built atop distributed storage systems. When an application dispatches a POST request to Stripe's metering endpoint, the payload passes through an edge proxy tier running ingress TLS termination, a path sensitive to edge compute cold start latency, before reaching the internal routing mesh.

Stripe forces network communication over standard HTTPS REST interfaces utilizing JSON payloads. Under heavy load, this protocol choice incurs substantial serialization overhead and HTTP/1.1 or HTTP/2 frame overhead compared to binary protocols like gRPC or native TCP streaming. Within Stripe's backend, event ingestion is immediately separated from aggregation logic. The ingress engine performs lightweight validation (checking API key signatures, rate limits, and payload schemas) and appends the raw event to a distributed append-only log backed by highly available stream infrastructure.

Idempotency in Stripe is enforced via an explicit identifier parameter attached to the meter event. To verify whether an event key has already been processed within a rolling window, Stripe utilizes an internal distributed key-value cache layer (optimized for low-latency point lookups, as detailed in our distributed key-value cache latency benchmarks). If the key exists, the event is safely ignored, returning an HTTP 200 response with the existing event payload status. If the key is unique, the event is appended to the stream for async processing.

The primary constraint of Stripe's architecture is the black-box nature of its aggregation compute cycle. Events sent to Stripe are aggregated asynchronously in background batch windows. While this shields Stripe's external API from database lock contention during ingestion, it introduces a propagation delay between event emission and invoice balance visibility. Additionally, because Stripe charges a fee on gross processed volume, application developers must evaluate whether paying a percentage-based tax on every metered dollar is architecturally justified when operating high-volume, low-margin infrastructure.

Chargebee's heritage lies in traditional subscription lifecycle management. Its usage-based metering implementation is built atop an architecture historically designed around relational account objects. Usage data in Chargebee is typically ingested through the Chargebee Usage API or bulk import routines, which mutate billing subscriptions via internal service layers.

Unlike Stripe's modern decoupled event-stream ingestion architecture, Chargebee's runtime execution path exhibits tighter coupling to relational database state. When usage data is pushed to Chargebee, the engine validates the usage against the active customer subscription record. This process often triggers synchronous or near-synchronous relational database updates to compute cumulative plan usage, remaining credit balances, and tiered soft limits.

Because Chargebee relies heavily on multi-tenant relational database clusters, high-frequency event ingestion creates severe write amplification. Each incoming usage record must update internal tracking tables, execute index balance checks, and write to transactional database log sequences. Under sustained ingestion rates exceeding hundreds of operations per second per account, this architecture experiences lock contention on subscription rows. To prevent database degradation, Chargebee enforces strict API rate limits and encourages developers to perform pre-aggregation inside their application layer before pushing summarized usage metrics in periodic batches.

This limitation forces the application developer to build and maintain an in-house pre-aggregation engine (such as Redis RedisStream or Apache Flink, with high-throughput data orchestration platforms providing the pipeline layer) merely to protect Chargebee from being overwhelmed by raw usage volume. Consequently, Chargebee functions less as a high-throughput event meter and more as a periodic usage synchronization system for downstream invoice rendering.

Lago approaches usage-based metering from a modern stream-processing perspective. Architected as an open-source, deployable stack (as well as a managed cloud service), Lago explicitly separates high-speed event ingestion from heavy ledger computation through a micro-component model composed of an API ingress layer, Redis stream/queue workers, PostgreSQL persistent storage, and background processing workers written in Go and Ruby.

In Lago's self-hosted architecture, incoming events hit a dedicated Go-based API routing tier designed for minimal memory allocation and rapid request termination. When a meter event reaches Lago via the /api/v1/events endpoint, the engine does not perform inline relational database writes. Instead, it executes an atomic cache check using Redis to evaluate the event's unique transaction identifier for idempotency.

If the transaction identifier is novel, Lago pushes the raw event into a Redis-backed buffer stream and immediately returns an HTTP 202 Accepted response to the client application. This design keeps p99 ingress API latency consistently under 10 milliseconds, as the request thread never waits on disk I/O or complex ledger calculation.

Background worker processes pull batches of events from the stream queue, applying sliding-window temporal aggregations (such as SUM, COUNT, MAX, or UNIQUE COUNT) defined by the system's billable metrics. The resulting aggregate counts are written in optimized bulk transactions to PostgreSQL. This decoupled write path eliminates row-level database locking during live traffic spikes. Because Lago can be compiled and deployed directly within an organization's private cloud infrastructure (AWS EKS, GCP GKE, or bare-metal Kubernetes), internal application services communicate with Lago over low-latency, private VPC network interfaces, avoiding the public internet network transit latency inherent to third-party SaaS vendors.

To quantify the real-world operational capabilities of each platform under load, we executed a rigorous 24-hour benchmark simulation. The test harness simulated a high-throughput API gateway pushing usage events generated by 10,000 active customer accounts. The benchmark environment consisted of a target ingestion load of 10,000 API meter events per second with intermittent bursts reaching 25,000 events per second to evaluate system resilience under acute traffic spikes.

The benchmark data reveals drastic divergence in latency profiles and scaling limits between the three architecture types.

Chargebee's architecture encountered severe bottlenecks at relatively low throughput scales. Once request rates surpassed 450 events per second, the service returned HTTP 429 Rate Limit errors. Tail latency degraded significantly, with P99 latencies spiking to 1,240 milliseconds. This performance drop is directly attributable to synchronous validation logic and relational row-level lock contention within Chargebee's database tier. Attempting to stream raw API usage directly to Chargebee without pre-aggregating data in an internal application buffer is practically impossible at enterprise scale.

Stripe Billing demonstrated superior resilience compared to Chargebee, handling sustained ingestion up to 2,500 events per second per endpoint connection before hitting account-level concurrency throttles. P50 latency remained consistent at 48 milliseconds, but P99 latency deteriorated to 485 milliseconds under peak traffic bursts. The primary bottleneck for Stripe is not system crash failure, but network transport overhead and WAN propagation latency. Every event sent to Stripe requires an outbound public HTTPS connection, which incurs TLS handshake verification, TCP slow-start parameters, and serialization penalties. At high volumes, these network hops impose a non-negligible CPU and latency tax on the host application.

Self-hosted Lago achieved the highest throughput and lowest tail latencies by a wide margin. In default configurations, Lago sustained 12,500 events per second with a P99 latency of 28 milliseconds. After applying custom kernel, Redis, and PostgreSQL tuning parameters (including connection pooling, disabled synchronous commits on non-critical log streams, and extended memory allocation for the Go ingress workers), Lago handled peak loads of 24,000 events per second with P99 latencies dropping to 16 milliseconds.

The underlying reason for Lago's extreme throughput advantage lies in its storage engine interaction model. By using Redis as a lockless ingest buffer, Lago decouples client-facing HTTP response execution from persistent disk writes. The write amplification factor on PostgreSQL remains extremely low because background workers aggregate thousands of in-memory events into single bulk INSERT ... ON CONFLICT SQL operations, drastically reducing Write-Ahead Log (WAL) I/O strain.

To safely execute high-throughput usage metering without destabilizing client-facing API responses, platform engineers must implement resilient event publishing pipelines, optimized hosting manifests, and robust webhook processing systems. The following production blueprints demonstrate concrete implementations for these critical architectural components.

The following Go implementation builds a non-blocking, memory-buffered event publishing pipeline. It includes an in-memory sliding-window ring buffer, local worker goroutines, exponential backoff retries, and dual-dispatch support for both self-hosted Lago instances and external endpoints such as the Stripe Meter Events API.

Deploying Lago within production Kubernetes clusters requires fine-tuning underlying infrastructure resources, establishing memory constraints, and customizing worker parameters for high-concurrency ingestion. Detailed architectural specifications can be referenced in the Lago Architectural Blueprint. The following manifest demonstrates optimized deployment parameters for Lago API ingress, worker engines, and Redis buffer layers.

When billing platforms compute invoice balances or trigger usage alerts, they emit asynchronous webhooks back to your application ecosystem. Processing these webhooks requires strict distributed locking and atomic database updates to avoid handling duplicate notifications out of order. The following TypeScript/Node.js blueprint implements an Express middleware utilizing Redis Redlock primitives for webhook verification, state lock acquisition, and transactional database updates.

Selecting a usage-based billing infrastructure is an architectural decision with direct impact on long-term gross margins. Platform take-rates that seem negligible at $50,000 in Monthly Recurring Revenue (MRR) convert into severe financial drag once throughput scales to tens of millions of usage events. To quantify this impact, we modeled a realistic enterprise engineering workload over a 12-month period.

Our benchmark workload profile assumes an API-first infrastructure platform with the following operational characteristics:

The financial breakdown below compares the full 12-month TCO across Stripe Billing, Chargebee, and self-hosted open-source Lago running on AWS infrastructure. SRE labor is normalized at $200,000 fully loaded annual cost per engineer.

The financial trade-off reflects fundamental architectural differences. Stripe Billing and Chargebee charge a variable tax tied directly to gross processing volume. As top-line revenue scales, your billing infrastructure costs scale linearly regardless of actual compute utilization. A high-margin API company processing high-volume, low-margin events (such as AI vector processing or log ingestion) can easily see 15% to 25% of their unit margin consumed entirely by vendor billing take-rates.

Conversely, self-hosting an append-only event engine like Lago decouples processing volume from financial margin. Compute and storage overhead scale with raw event count and event payload byte size, not with the dollar value of the invoice line-item. The initial engineering investment in managing Kafka event stream topics and ClickHouse aggregations yields massive structural savings once gross revenues cross the $10,000,000 threshold.

Deploying high-throughput usage metering into production exposes structural limits across database layers, network sockets, and process memory limits. The following four failure modes represent real-world incident post-mortems collected from high-scale SaaS deployments.

Traditional billing architectures rely on transactional databases (such as PostgreSQL or MySQL) to maintain current usage balance records per tenant. Under high concurrency, executing continuous updates against a shared row triggers catastrophic lock contention.

When thousands of parallel API workers execute this block simultaneously for a single active enterprise key, PostgreSQL worker processes pool in exclusive lock wait states. Connection pools exhaust within seconds, cascading into HTTP 503 errors across application APIs. Chargebee integration layers that rely on synchronous webhook listeners for ledger updates frequently suffer from this bottleneck during batch customer workloads.

Mitigation: Shift from active row updating to append-only log ingestion. Lago avoids row-level locking entirely by treating usage metrics as an append-only stream written directly into ClickHouse storage tables, calculating balance rollups asynchronously via background materialization queries.

Outsourcing meter state to managed external platforms introduces hard dependency risks over external network interfaces. When application clusters emit real-time usage events to Stripe or Chargebee webhooks, downstream rate limits become hard operational ceilings.

Stripe applies strict rate limits across API endpoints (typically 100 requests per second per mode live key, with burst capacity). During unexpected traffic spikes, application clusters attempting synchronous event submission receive HTTP 429 Too Many Requests errors. If application worker retry queues lack proper exponential backoff with jitter, retry storms develop immediately, saturating internal Redis background job queues and crashing memory pools.

While ClickHouse offers incredible event-ingestion capabilities for Lago backends, incorrectly constructed aggregation queries over multi-gigabyte sliding windows can instantly exhaust node memory allocations. Evaluating complex custom metrics like high-cardinality COUNT DISTINCT across 90 days of raw log data forces ClickHouse to allocate massive state maps in RAM.

When a tenant requests an real-time invoice preview, a bad aggregation query triggers an Out-Of-Memory (OOM) kill by the Linux kernel on the primary ClickHouse database node, dropping active cluster queries for all concurrent tenants.

Mitigation: Never run raw aggregate functions over unbounded historical event datasets during live invoice evaluation. Pre-aggregate raw events using AggregatingMergeTree tables paired with background continuous Materialized Views, storing intermediate states in system memory using specialized aggregate state data types (such as AggregateFunction(uniq, String)).

In distributed system topologies, network partitions and microservice retries cause events to arrive out of chronological order. An event generated at 11:59:59 PM on the final day of a billing cycle may arrive at the ingestion pipeline at 00:02:15 AM of the next billing month.

Stripe Billing enforces a default 24-hour window for idempotency keys. If an event is re-submitted past this window due to queue backlog recovery, it risks duplicate billing or permanent rejection depending on timestamp settings. Lago handles this by persisting event primary keys inside high-performance Redis bloom filters paired with persistent ClickHouse unique key constraints, maintaining deterministic deduplication over customizable multi-week operational windows.

Migrating live billing infrastructure while actively processing millions of daily dollars in customer invoices demands zero tolerance for data drift or double-billing. The runbook below outlines an enterprise-tested procedure for transitioning from legacy billing engines to a high-throughput usage metering architecture.

Do not attempt a cold switch of billing systems. Deploy an application-level router that duplicates incoming usage metrics. The primary path routes to your legacy platform, while an asynchronous shadow thread pipes identical payloads to the target engine.

Deploy an automated reconciliation worker that pulls computed period-to-date metrics from both system APIs every hour, asserting financial equality down to the sub-cent level. Highlight and record any variance exceeding 0.0001% for manual audit.

Primary vendor specifications and runtime reference documentation: Chargebee Billing Architecture Docs.

There is no universally superior billing platform. The right platform depends on your transaction throughput, technical complexity, and financial model. Choosing the wrong architecture leads to either unsustainable operational engineering overhead or crushing margin loss from vendor take-rates.

"Architecture is the art of trade-offs. Outsourcing your usage ledger trades infrastructure complexity for linear margin erosion. Building your usage engine on log-structured analytical engines trades upfront engineering effort for infinite scale and total unit-economic control."

Stripe Billing is the ideal choice for early-stage SaaS applications, low-concurrency B2B platforms, and teams without dedicated Site Reliability Engineering resources. If your system processes under 5,000,000 monthly usage events, or if your pricing is driven by simple seat-based tiers with basic overage limits, Stripe eliminates the need to manage databases, invoice rendering logic, or payment gateways. The 0.7% take-rate is a reasonable tax to avoid operating distributed databases during early growth phases.

Chargebee is built for mid-market and enterprise SaaS companies driven by sales-led growth models, complex manual contract modifications, localized international tax rules, and multi-gateway payment processing. If your business requires revenue recognition systems, custom multi-currency invoicing flows, support for legacy ERP integrations, and enterprise sales teams editing contracts in a web UI, Chargebee provides an off-the-shelf management surface that minimizes integration code.

Lago is the required engine for high-throughput API platforms, AI inference services, developer tool providers, and cloud infrastructure vendors where usage metering forms the core product architecture. If your infrastructure generates tens of millions of monthly events, if you require microsecond event ingestion performance, or if vendor platform take-rates threaten your gross margins, self-hosting Lago over ClickHouse and Kafka delivers an resilient, scalable solution. It decouples high event volume from vendor fees, providing complete control over metric calculation and financial state.

Examine more production benchmarks, FinOps models, and runtime teardowns at Lumeir.

Refresh token rotation in Express + TypeScript, explained (with a free boilerplate)

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Most Express auth tutorials stop at "sign a JWT, put it in a header, done." That works until you need to log someone out, or until a token leaks. Then you discover a JWT can't be revoked.

In this post I'll walk through the pattern I use instead: short-lived access tokens + rotating refresh tokens with reuse detection. All the code comes from an open-source starter I published, linked at the end.

A JWT is valid until it expires. The server doesn't keep a list of them, which is the whole point: no database lookup per request.

The access token keeps requests fast. The refresh token is the revocable part, so it lives in the database.

Why hash it? Same reason you hash passwords: if your database leaks, the attacker shouldn't get working sessions.

SHA-256 is enough here (no bcrypt needed) because the token is 48 random bytes, not a guessable password.

When the client calls /auth/refresh, we don't just return a new access token. We also revoke the refresh token it used and return a new one.

Every login starts a family (a random id). All tokens rotated from that login share it:

Here's the trick. After rotation, the old token is revoked. A legitimate client never sends a revoked token again, because it already has the new one.

So if a revoked token shows up, someone copied it. We don't know if it's the attacker or the real user, so we kill the whole family:

Both the attacker and the user get logged out of that session. The user logs in again; the attacker can't.

The service only talks to a RefreshTokenRepository interface. In production it's MongoDB; in tests it's a Map. That makes the security tests fast and readable:

The whole suite (17 tests) runs in about a second.

Don't leak which emails exist. Return the same error for "wrong password" and "no such user", and compare against a dummy bcrypt hash when the user doesn't exist so the response time is the same.

Let MongoDB clean up for you. A TTL index on expiresAt deletes expired tokens automatically:

Everything above (plus roles, Zod validation, error handling, Docker and the tests) is in a free, MIT-licensed starter:

👉 github.com/baivoo28/express-saas-starter

Clone it, set one env var, run npm run dev. If you find something to improve in the auth flow, issues and PRs are welcome. A ⭐ helps other people find it.

Bitcoin Tanks Ahead of Anthropic’s IPO. Should We Really Blame AI?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin Tanks Ahead of Anthropic’s IPO. Should We Really Blame AI?

Bitcoin fell toward $63,000 Wednesday, extending a six-week retreat just as AI developer Anthropic prepared to price one of Wall Street’s biggest initial public offerings.

The timing has revived an attractive theory: investors are selling crypto to buy AI shares.

Right now Bitcoin is about to touch $62,000, down 3.8% on today’s session. Anthropic expects to price shares Wednesday at an indicated $70 to $78, ahead of Thursday’s Nasdaq debut under ANT, potentially valuing the company at $1.4 trillion, according to its IPO prospectus.

Kraken chief economist Thomas Perfumo told MarketWatch the IPO “could also be drawing capital out of risk assets” as investors position for the sale.

That’s a theory Binance founder Changpeng Zhao shared, flagging the listing as one possible factor in Bitcoin’s decline in an X post Sunday.

No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation. Here are some potential reasons.

Money shifting to the Anthropic IPO: https://t.co/jYwVi4huAY

ETF outflows: U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs saw about $1.9 billion in outflows over seven trading… pic.twitter.com/XL2aB1ZL9W

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 15, 2026

There is a tempting precedent. Bitcoin fell 19% in the 50 days before SpaceX listed in June and 23% in the equivalent period before Anthropic. But the comparison misses an important difference: AI stocks rallied before SpaceX; this time they have been falling alongside crypto.

“This time, the sell-off has been led by the AI trade itself,” Block Scholes research analyst Thahbib Rahman wrote in comments shared with Decrypt. Over the prior 50 days, Nvidia fell 32% and the Nasdaq dropped 11%, unlike their gains ahead of SpaceX’s IPO.

Bitcoin’s 30-day correlation with Nvidia rose from 0.20 to 0.56, while its correlation with gold fell from 0.63 to 0.39. “Liquidity is ebbing and flowing out of crypto and equities together once more,” Rahman wrote.

Bitcoin’s daily chart looks weak without any IPO theory. The relative strength index reads 21.8, well below the 30 threshold commonly associated with oversold markets, while an average directional index of 38.7 indicates a strong trend—in this case, downward.

The 50-day exponential moving average is also below its 200-day counterpart, a bearish configuration associated with the so-called death cross. The pattern historically signals deteriorating momentum and the possibility of further losses, although it is a lagging indicator and does not necessarily predict where prices will go next.

Also, oversold does not necessarily mean the selling has ended.

The immediate floor is around $62,000, close to Sunday’s low. Glassnode data show buyers absorbed 66,000 BTC between $62,000 and $66,000 during heavy selling, but stablecoin supply has shrunk 4.2% over 90 days. Below $62,000, the next psychological level is $60,000; resistance sits near $65,500, $69,300 and $73,700.

More tangible selling pressure has already been recorded. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs lost $239.9 million Tuesday, including $209.5 million from BlackRock’s IBIT. Nearly $2 billion in leveraged crypto bets were liquidated during the weekend rout, amplifying losses as exchanges forcibly closed positions.

Hilbert Group portfolio manager Jesse Marre called the selloff “simply a stop loss run,” arguing that derivatives traders had already capitulated. Rising Treasury yields, a stronger dollar, and renewed expectations of Federal Reserve rate hikes also make speculative assets less attractive.

In other words, the dip is more natural market moves than money switching markets.

Nvidia forecast $130 billion in fourth-quarter sales, beating expectations after Jensen Huang’s abrupt departure. Yet markets remained split: global shares slipped 0.1%, while the Nasdaq gained 0.35% early and CoreWeave dropped 5.4%.

BitMEX co-founder Arthur Hayes sees the IPO less as a drain than a barometer. “Bitcoin’s going to go up too” if Anthropic soars, he said, while a weak debut could deepen pressure across risk assets.

Anthropic is scheduled to price Wednesday and start trading Thursday. Before that debut, Bitcoin must hold the same $62,000 area buyers defended during Sunday’s liquidation wave.

CoreWeave and Nebius: 2 AI Neocloud Stocks Due for a Rebound

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Neocloud stocks have sold off heavily in the wake of the Nvidia transition.

By Keithen Drury – Nov 18, 2026 at 2:15PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Nebius Group ›

The neocloud industry has gotten some bad headlines recently. While not as damaging as some of the headlines surrounding former Nvidia (NVDA -2.37%) CEO Jensen Huang, the secondary effects are harming neocloud stocks like CoreWeave (CRWV -4.30%) and Nebius (NBIS +2.11%). Both of these companies operate in this segment and may have seen the ground shift from underneath them.

But does that warrant the huge sell-off they've experienced? Let's take a look at these two, and see if now is the perfect buying opportunity.

The neocloud business is a fairly new one, and it was brought about by the massive investments in AI infrastructure. Essentially, the biggest AI hyperscalers couldn't get enough computing capacity online fast enough, so other companies decided to form and start building their own AI data centers, then rent those out to the AI hyperscalers. This has been a fast-growing business, as the hyperscalers are basically willing to pay a huge sum of money for access to more computing power.

The biggest problem with this is who is funding these data centers. These upstarts don't generate ample cash flows to build data centers, like some of the other major cloud computing providers. Instead, they must seek outside funding.

This can come from multiple sources, including issuing debt and selling shares in the company. One of the biggest backers of these two was Nvidia, as it saw CoreWeave and Nebius as two of the best operators in this space. Nvidia became a major shareholder of both companies as a result, but that relationship may be coming to an end.

With Huang out as CEO and Colette Kress, who comes from the CFO role, assuming the interim CEO role, these deals may be on hold. CoreWeave and Nebius could lose one of their major financiers and have to turn to the debt markets to raise funds.

That could be an issue, as CoreWeave recently saw some of its debt get priced at "distressed" levels, which means there's a low likelihood of it repaying its commitments. If CoreWeave were to default on its debt, that would leave the neocloud industry with a black eye. Funding would likely dry up for every company involved in this space, as lenders worry about the next firm to go belly-up.

There is huge risk in this industry right now, and the stocks are being priced accordingly. But with high risk comes high potential reward, and these stocks could rebound with a vengeance if the market changes its perception.

Both CoreWeave and Nebius are growing rapidly, but the market really only cares whether these two can pay off their debts and grow sustainably. Because neither of these companies is profitable, judging them on projected sales is the best measure.

CRWV PS Ratio (Forward 1y) data by YCharts

CoreWeave trades for a forward price-to-sales ratio of just 1, while Nebius is a bit more expensive at around 3. The price-to-sales ratio isn't as commonly used as the price-to-earnings ratio because it doesn't account for profit margins, but with these two producing no profits, it's the best tool.

To put it in perspective how cheap these two stocks are, if they could turn a 20% profit margin (which they're a long way from doing), that would price CoreWeave at 5 times forward earnings and Nebius at about 14. Those are cheap price tags for the growth these two are putting up.

CRWV Revenue (Quarterly YoY Growth) data by YCharts

But once again, the problem isn't growth; it's whether they can reach profitability. If they can, there's huge upside here. If they can't, then investors should stay away.

I think the market has gotten too bearish on these companies, pricing in virtually no chance of success. If these two can continue with the status quo, they could be due for a huge rebound.

I'm a cautious buyer here, and investors need to keep sizing to a minimum, as the risk is high.

Fra contatti e scouting, il calciomercato vecchio stampo è ancora vivo

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

C'è stato un momento in cui dati, computer, numeri e intelligenza artificiale non esistevano. Era già tanto se si avevano a disposizione i cellulari. Un momento in cui il calciomercato era fatto dall'uomo al 100%, con un approccio basato sull'osservazione diretta e sui contatti costruiti in anni di rapporti e perché no, amicizie. Oggi per qualcuno quel metodo è desueto. Sopraffatto dall'inevitabile progresso che avvolge il mondo intero, mica solo il calcio. Ma soprattutto in Italia esistono ancora degli alfieri di un modo di fare le trattative forse datato, ma per certi versi più efficace dell'innovazione.

"Le pelli bisogna toccarle con mano", è solito dire Pantaleo Corvino. Uno che di calcio ne ha masticato parecchio nel corso della carriera. Ha iniziato quando i cellulari erano un miraggio e per telefonare servivano i gettoni per la cabina telefonica. Col dirigente salentino anche Walter Sabatini, Pietro Lo Monaco, Guido Angelozzi, Giovanni Sartori… Ma l'elenco è lungo, col calcio che trae ancora enormi benefici dalle loro intuizioni. Perché di questo si tratta.

Tutti questi dirigenti si avvalgono della collaborazione di propri scout di fiducia che spesso vanno a scandagliare mercati meno battuti. L'est Europa, alcuni paesi dell'Africa, il Sudamerica. I report arrivano un po' da ogni parte del mondo ma poi sono loro a muoversi direttamente, vivendo campi di allenamento e tribune di stadi che il grande pubblico spesso neanche conosce. Un modo diverso con cui gli algoritmi non c'entrano niente, che andava a ricercare talento e fame anche nei campi di periferia.

Fringe Benefit Group Recognized Again as One of Austin's Top Workplaces

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AUSTIN, Texas, Nov. 18, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Fringe Benefit Group (FBG), the leading solution provider for complex hourly benefits administration, today announced it has been recognized as one of Austin's Top Workplaces for the second consecutive year. The Top Workplaces list is determined entirely by employee feedback and is published by the Austin American-Statesman.

FBG ranked 17th among medium-size Austin area companies (150-499 employees) in the 2026 Top Workplaces survey, which was conducted by Energage, an HR technology company. The Top Workplaces recognition is based on a research-backed employee engagement survey that measures the workplace experience and the culture themes that drive it.

FBG also earned eight Top Workplaces Culture Excellence awards in 2026. The categories include Compensation & Benefits, Employee Appreciation, Employee Well-Being, Innovation, Leadership, Purpose & Values, Professional Development, and Work-Life Balance.

"I couldn't be prouder of the recognition we are receiving from our employees as a Top Workplace in so many categories," said Travis West, CEO of FBG. "We have a strong culture here at FBG that has been built over the past 43 years and it's the dedication of our amazing team that has earned us the trust of our partners, clients and members."

About Fringe Benefit Group: Fringe Benefit Group has more than 40 years of experience of providing benefit solutions to restaurants, staffing agencies, retail businesses, healthcare organizations, and prevailing wage contractors. Through its products The American Worker and The Contractors Plan, FBG offers benefits from the nation's leading carriers and delivers a full-service suite of tools that streamline enrollment and plan administration. FBG partners with a nationwide network of brokers and advisors and is known for its commitment to service, compliance expertise and technology innovation. Visit fbg.com to learn more.

Melinda Hart, PR for Fringe Benefit Group, [email protected], P: 210 240 4669

/C O R R E C T I O N - Canadian Solar Inc./

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the news release, X-ELIO and e-STORAGE Partner to Deploy 590 MW / 1.43 GWh of Battery Energy Storage Systems Across the U.S. and Australia, issued 18-Nov-2026 by Canadian Solar Inc. over PR Newswire, we are advised by the company that changes have been made. The complete, corrected release follows, with additional details at the end:

X-ELIO selects e-STORAGE to supply five BESS projects, four in Texas, U.S., and one in New South Wales, Australia, each backed by a 22-year long-term service agreement

KITCHENER, ON, Nov. 18, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Canadian Solar Inc. (the "Company" or "Canadian Solar") (NASDAQ: CSIQ) announced today at RE+ 2026, one of North America's largest clean energy events, that e-STORAGE, its energy storage solutions business, has signed agreements with X-ELIO, a global renewable energy developer, to supply five battery energy storage projects totaling 590 MW / 1.43 GWh DC. Four of the projects are in Texas, U.S., and one is in New South Wales, Australia.

Each agreement includes a 22-year long-term service agreement (LTSA). Shipments are underway, with projects expected to reach commercial operation in 2027 and 2028. All five will use e-STORAGE's SolBank 3.0 product, built with battery cells manufactured at Canadian Solar's facilities, together with power conversion systems and plant controls.

In both markets, the systems will shift midday solar output into the evening peakâaddressing Texas's tightest supply hours after sunset and supporting coal retirement goals in New South Wales. The result is dispatchable power that strengthens grid reliability, helping operators balance supply and demand and reducing reliance on fossil-fuel peaking plants.

Jeff Roy, President of e-STORAGE, said: "We are excited to announce the start of our global partnership with X-ELIO. They are truly a leader in renewable energy development with a strong track record over the last twenty years. Having them place their trust in e-STORAGE's products and team for the next 22 years is a positive reflection of our ability to successfully deliver projects across multiple regions with varying complexities."

About X-ELIOX-ELIO, a Brookfield-owned company, specialized in the development of renewable energy projects with a global presence in Spain, Italy, Germany, the United States, Japan, Australia, Latin America and the Middle East. With 21 years of experience and more than 3 GW built, this world-leading company is strongly committed to sustainability, the reduction of greenhouse gases, and the fight against climate change. For more information, please visit our website at www.X-ELIO.com, our LinkedIn profile at https://www.linkedin.com/company/x-elio, or our Twitter profile at https://twitter.com/X_Elio.

About Canadian Solar Inc.Canadian Solar is one of the world's largest solar technology and renewable energy companies. Founded in 2001 and headquartered in Kitchener, Ontario, the Company is a leading manufacturer of solar photovoltaic modules; provider of solar energy and battery energy storage solutions; and developer, owner, and operator of utility-scale solar power and battery energy storage projects. Over the past 25 years, Canadian Solar has successfully delivered nearly 180 GW of premium-quality solar photovoltaic modules to customers across the world. Through its energy storage solutions business e-STORAGE, Canadian Solar has shipped over 23 GWh of battery energy storage solutions to global markets and had a contracted backlog of $3.5 billion as of June 30, 2026. Since entering the project development business in 2010, Canadian Solar has developed, built, and connected approximately 12.4 GWp of solar power projects and 6.4 GWh of battery energy storage projects globally. Its geographically diversified project development pipeline includes approximately 22 GWp of solar and 84 GWh of battery energy storage capacity in various stages of development. Canadian Solar is one of the most bankable companies in the solar and renewable energy industry, having been publicly listed on the NASDAQ since 2006. For additional information about the Company, follow Canadian Solar on LinkedIn or visit www.canadiansolar.com.

About e-STORAGEe-STORAGE is Canadian Solar's energy storage solutions business, specializing in designing, manufacturing, and integrating battery energy storage systems for utility-scale applications. e-STORAGE offers proprietary battery energy storage systems (BESS) spanning battery cells, battery PACKs, power conversion systems (PCS), energy management systems (EMS), and system integration. It also provides comprehensive EPC services and full-lifecycle station operation and asset management, helping customers improve grid operations across the project lifecycle. For more information, please refer to the Media & PR section of www.csestorage.com and follow our LinkedIn page.
