How can districts keep CTE programs relevant as tech evolves?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The rapid pace of change can make it difficult for even employers to have clear answers for workplace readiness, an industry expert says.

How can leaders of high school career and technical education programs ensure those offerings remain relevant in preparing students to adapt in a rapidly changing workforce?

“That question is one everyone is trying to answer right now. I’m not sure there is one clear answer,” said Alisha Hyslop, chief policy, research and content officer at the Association for Career and Technical Education.

Hyslop said the main piece of advice for educators is to work closely with businesses and industries to ensure programs are preparing students with the skills they’ll need to be successful — and that’s probably the same, at least on the surface, as it would have been 10 or 20 years ago.

But there are important distinctions in 2026, Hyslop said.

Because technology is changing so rapidly, employers don’t always have a clear answer for workplace readiness approaches, she said.

Hyslop said ACTE is also hearing that students need to understand basic, foundational artificial intelligence skills and how to use them. Additionally, students should continue to "build up durable skills," which is "really critical," she said.

Despite employers’ own lack of clarity at times, regular conversations should definitely continue, Hyslop said.

“There should be a give-and-take between the CTE educators, and folks working on curriculum and instruction in CTE, and the employer community,” she said. And although technology will continue to rapidly evolve, “students are still going to need critical thinking and communications skills, and the ability to work in teams, and evaluate information — and all of those foundational skills that apply across technologies.”

CTE programs focused on information technology are changing the most rapidly, although Hyslop said she has seen plenty of research to show that across the spectrum of career fields, new AI tools are being implemented and deployed. One example is the use of the technology in the agriculture field to monitor soil health and predict crop growth, she said.

“We had some examples from a school in Bentonville [Ark.] that was using AI in health science programs. They had created a meal planning app for mothers with gestational diabetes, or for pregnant women who could get answers to specific questions; those were joint projects between healthcare and technology students," Hyslop said. "There were lots of applications in digital media, and lots of ethical considerations that students are exploring as they go through that process.”

While some industries are more impacted by the advent of AI and other changes in the workplace than others, at first glance, people might think it’s mainly an issue in technology classes, Hyslop said. “And that is not the case. Because we’re seeing applications across career areas, once students get into the workplace.”

During a panel discussion at a recent ACTE conference focused on the use of AI in CTE programs, Hyslop said, students emphasized how important it was that they learned foundational skills first.

One student pointed out the approach of using a calculator to solve math problems, she recalled. “But that’s not how we teach students in kindergarten. We teach them basic mathematical functions first, and then introduce the calculator to do more complicated things.”

By learning the foundational level skills in CTE programs, they could evaluate the answers given by AI more competently, the students said. For instance, a student studying automotive mechanics could examine AI-generated instructions on how to fix cars.

“If you had no real understanding of those instructions, you wouldn’t know if it made a mistake,” she said. “That was a really important takeaway from that panel — that even students realized that AI, or whatever the next technology is, is still going to need people to instruct, evaluate and modify all of these technologies.”

Why 2 Pinterest vets and a neurosurgeon are building a Reddit for doctors

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Medicine’s complexity and ever-evolving nature means doctors will always have more to learn and discuss—but in the digital age, there’s also a question of where those conversations can best take place.

Online, healthcare workers’ discourse is distributed across various social media platforms, forums, and group chats. Meanwhile, offline, medical professionals are “hyper-specialized,” with limited opportunities to work with colleagues from other disciplines, according to Dr. Rohan Ramakrishna, chief of neurosurgery at New York-Presbyterian Brooklyn Methodist Hospital.

As a neurosurgeon who specializes in brain tumors, he said, “the doctors I interact with on a regular basis are radiation oncologists and neuro-oncologists, and that’s it.” Even hospital doctors’ lounges are disappearing, he added, removing another opportunity for doctors to socialize and discuss medicine.

So Ramakrishna teamed up with Vikram Bhaskaran and Arun Ranganathan—two Pinterest alums who previously led engineering teams related to creators, content, and partners—to cofound an app that could foster that sense of community. 

Initially, the trio envisioned Roon as a place for doctors to provide patients with information; but in the app’s early days, Bhaskaran said, they found that physicians were especially interested in seeing what their own peers had to say—so they decided to rework the platform to facilitate those interactions.

Now, a year after its launch, the platform is adding features that bring Pinterest’s focus on sharing to the thousands of physicians on the platform to share everything from treatment advice to conference photos. As social media apps shift and online misinformation proliferates, the founders are hoping Roon will help physicians separate the signal from the noise.

Where doctors gather online

In some ways, Roon’s concept echoes that of #MedTwitter, which became a widely used, informal hub for medical experts and others to exchange information, ideas, and opinions, creating “a completely open-source environment that democratizes medical information,” as two physicians wrote in 2021. The hashtag still exists, and some continue to use it, but Twitter’s transition into X—and the accompanying changes—scattered some of  #MedTwitter to other platforms.

But medical science is always evolving, Ramakrishna said, as are politics and policy changes, and doctors lack a “home” for making sense of it all. Roon is “a place where you have net new knowledge creation, because people are trying to unpack what it all means, and understand it together.” 

To help shape Roon’s knowledge-creation network, the company enlisted more than 400 “founding physicians” from a range of specialties and institutions, who the company said “are owners with equity in Roon.” 

“A year ago, we had kind of a thesis: that physicians should have a place online where they can collaborate, connect, and that was safe and private,” said Bhaskaran, and it’s “been really heartening” to see how physicians are now using Roon. 

Creating community for a core audience

Roon joins a growing number of communications platforms designed for clinicians. Doximity, a networking service that offers an AI scribe and other tools, says more than 85% of U.S. physicians are registered members. There’s also Sermo, Figure 1, Mednet, Doquity—which is used by clinicians across much of Asia—and other new ventures like Marit Health, where medical professionals can share salary data. 

Each of them varies in form and function. Roon, which has the benefit of being designed by Pinterest veterans, is built to look and feel like mainstream social media sites. On it, users discuss new research, policy changes, and health topics that emerge in the news cycle, Ramakrishna said, and ask for advice on patient care. For example, he said, a family medicine doctor who posed a question related to reproductive health and the pituitary gland got replies from peers in family medicine, OB-GYN, high-risk maternal-fetal medicine, neurosurgery and neurology. 

There’s also an ability to form private groups—for example, Ramakrishna said, there’s one for chief medical officers, and another for residential program directors—and a new, Pinterest-board-inspired “Collections” feature that lets users group related content and media together. 

Selective, intentionally

Roon also differs from most medical communications apps in that, for the time being, it’s exclusive to physicians—M.D.s and D.O.s—who are licensed in the U.S.

Bhaskaran says the criteria mean that Roon can function as a place to share expertise built in the field—something that augments published literature in an important way. 

“There’s so much of medicine that’s outside of the published literature, that’s not accessible via chatbot or via AI: that’s in people’s heads,” he says. 

Roon currently has tens of thousands of users, Bhaskaran says, noting the goal is to reach 250,000 by the end of next year. That would constitute almost a quarter of the 1.08 million physicians that were licensed in the U.S. as of 2024. 

Part of the challenge of growing is the fragmentation of physicians across various social media apps. Some physicians also say they value the diverse perspectives offered by other healthcare workers, like nurses and pharmacists, and by those in other countries. 

They do plan to invite certain non-physician specialties, like infectious disease scientists, Bhaskaran added, but they still have work to do to build up the community of physicians, which remains their first goal. They also plan to expand Roon internationally, “but again, very thoughtfully, because verification is just a little bit of a hard problem once you leave the United States.” In the meantime, the company has a waitlist for medical professionals who’d like to join.

Human amid AI

Roon currently employs AI for tasks like filling out physicians’ profiles, routing users’ questions to specialists, and generating summaries of content. Its team is also working on new AI-powered functions to keep users up-to-date, like an audio digest feature that summarizes medical news and related Roon posts.

Then, there’s the training question: a database of medical information based on real-life expertise would be valuable for training LLMs, and physicians on X have expressed concern about it being used that way. A company spokesperson said Roon “currently has no plans to sell its data,” though the company may use it to refine its own models, and hasn’t made a “definitive commitment ruling out all future uses involving third-party LLMs.” 

But in the face of AI’s all-consuming nature, “our thesis is actually [that] the human network will become more valuable over time, especially as most of the world goes, ‘hey, we’re unclear who wrote this content,’ or ‘everything you see on the internet is AI slop,'” Bhaskaran says. ”We’re taking kind of an old school bet—which is, there’ll be value for community, and that’ll transcend any specific technology.”

‘No one asked for this’: AI is being pushed on people—and they’re sick of it

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The AI era is fully here—and while companies bill it as a solution to problems, caveats like mass layoffs, environmental effects, privacy concerns and the sloppification of culture have many people fed up with something they never desired in the first place.

The technology faces growing resentment from workers, who are being urged to embrace AI tools even as companies have rushed to reduce their workforces in favor of automation. 

Online, many posts about AI have a common refrain: no one asked for these tools or the disruptions they’re causing. 

“Nobody asked for Google to be worse—but AI made it happen,” one X user recently remarked. “No one asked for computer hardware costs to rise—but AI made it happen. No one asked for human customer service to be replaced—but AI made it happen.”

In another post, Sarah Hoyt, a science fiction, fantasy and mystery author, said she doesn’t hate AI, but is “begging all the crazy people out there to stop pushing AI where no one asked for it.”

The disenchantment plays out in the data: In 2019, 81% of Glassdoor reviews mentioning AI were positive, the company said, compared to 43% this year. Meanwhile, Pew Research found that 52% of Americans were more concerned than excited about AI, up from 37% in 2021; and in an Ipsos poll from June, 67% of respondents thought AI would worsen the worker experience.

“The closest analog is the replacement of physical labor throughout the long Industrial Revolution,” said Jiemin Tina Wei, an assistant professor of history at Florida International University. But in this case, it’s “brain work,” rather than manual labor, that’s being replaced. 

“And now, the industries that are getting allegedly wiped out are the ‘low-hanging fruit’ of white-collar work,” she said.

But livelihoods being gutted isn’t the only reason many are anti-AI.

The productivity paradox

Louis Hyman, a historian at Johns Hopkins University who specializes in labor and the American economy, points to a “productivity paradox”: a contradiction that was also generally true of the rise of computers in the ‘80s, in which similarly dizzying investments didn’t necessarily make people better at their jobs as promised.

Hyman points to snags like the frequency of software updates: that “every time you learn how to do something, suddenly they break it, and you have to relearn it.” With the introduction of AI, it’s “as if all software is being updated, all at once, everywhere.” 

Another part of the equation, he said, is that it isn’t clear how some people are using AI tools.

Hyman said he uses AI to write software and automate “boring tasks”—for example, he wrote a script to rotate the thousands of photos he takes in archives so they all face right-side-up. But, he said, “most people are not wanting to sit down and write Python code to automate their job. They just want to do their job.” 

Added to that is the deep fear of being replaced once the work is automated.

“I’m a tenured professor—I literally can’t be fired,” he said. “If I automate some boring part of my job, that just means I have more time to do the more interesting parts of my job. And that’s not true for all people, right?” 

A new occupational hazard

Another reason many people push back on AI is the lack of adequate guardrails—especially when it could cause major issues at work.

In one case, an AI tool deleted a software company’s entire database. In another, AI was blamed for deleting an entirely edited season of a Swedish reality show mere weeks before the scheduled premiere. In medical settings, AI scribes have put inaccurate information in patient records, raising the question of who would be held liable for resulting medical errors. 

Meanwhile, misreads by AI identification tools used by law enforcement have led to wrongful arrests and injuries, per the MIT AI Risk Initiative. Other AI-generated errors have popped up in court documents, federal reports, news stories and fast food drive-thrus. 

“The main point I’ve pushed and will continue to push re: AI is ‘no one asked for this,’ ” radio host Adam Xii said on X, in response to news that Anthropic hallucinated details in their defense during a music copyright lawsuit. “So when a product or service no one asked for suddenly becomes ubiquitous, one must ask: why? Because AI tools are meant to generate more wealth for the wealthy at the expense of creatives.”

At the least, AI’s convenience is being offset by a need to check its work that has created new opportunities for human error. The proliferation of “workslop” even led a tech company’s CEO to fully ban AI use, as Inc. reported this summer. 

“A lot of what I’ve seen is kind of annoyance at the proselytizers at work—or the people who are heavy adopters—creating a lot of extra work for the people around them,” because those people downstream then have to check the work, said Wei.

Wei said that content moderation and data annotation work “can be extremely mentally taxing.” Research has found that “good” challenging work will be both difficult and interesting to certain extents, she said. But checking output is both difficult, because you have to look for every small potential error, and boring.

“You’re not producing knowledge, but merely supervising the production of words, knowledge, content,” Wei said.

The unwanted technological advances continue

AI’s intrusive nature—accompanied, again, by the threat of replacement—is reflected in workers’ behavior, not just in fed-up tweetstorms. One report found that nearly a third of workers surveyed, and 44% among Gen Z, had tried to sabotage their companies’ AI strategies.

Section, an AI planning and upskilling company,  recently reported that 69% of workers surveyed said their organization had encouraged the use of AI agents, but only 16% actually used them. Thirty-eight percent said they use other AI features.

The frustration hasn’t seemed to deter tech companies.

“We heard you wanted Copilot Mode at work,” read an X post from Microsoft that promoted Edge’s Copilot Mode for Business.

“No, you heard wrong,” a computer technician replied. “Literally no one asked for all this AI. In fact, everyone wants to know how to remove it.” 

Questions of organizations

In some cases, it seems companies are starting to discover that automation has some shortcomings.

In the U.S., 32% of hiring managers who eliminated positions in favor of AI have had to reinstate the same or similar roles, according to research from consulting firm Robert Half. Similarly, Gartner, Inc. predicts that by 2027, half of companies that attributed reductions in staff to AI will rehire people to perform similar work.

Wei said the mismatch between the hopes for AI and the reality of its adoption relates to a quote from Bill Haywood, a famous labor organizer, who said that “the manager’s brains are under the workman’s cap.”

In other words, she said, “it’s really ultimately the workers who possess the knowledge and the know-how to run day-to-day operations. Some people have argued that the history of labor is just managers trying to take back that knowledge in an attempt to replace workers in the production.”

“And I guess some people experience AI that way—that it’s being [imposed] upon them so that they train, ultimately, their final, automated replacement.”

Hyman said that a better alternative to the “adapt or die” mentality would be for organizations to use the technology to empower their employees. They should “actually say, ‘okay, we’re gonna create organizational conditions that let you replace yourself, and then focus on the work that needs to be done’—because the amount of work to be done is not finite, right?”

“I get told on a nearly weekly basis that historians are going to be replaced,” Hyman said. “So, yeah: it depends on how valuable you think getting reliable information and history is.”

He added that a world “where the only source of history or journalism is from either Open AI or Anthropic” would feel like an “Orwellian nightmare.”

“That seems not a great road to go down,” he said.

Boy George on His Controversial Israel Trip, Ed Sheeran and the Friends He Lost Along the Way

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Culture Club singer names the former collaborator who told him “Shame on you” and backs Robert Kraft’s decision to bar Macklemore from Gillette Stadium: “You can take your freedom of speech over there and do it on the street.”

Boy George wrote his new single, “We Will Dance Again,” after visiting the Nova Exhibition in London, an immersive installation that commemorates the Oct. 7, 2023, attacks on the Nova Music Festival that resulted in the deaths of 378 people and 44 people taken hostage.

He did it, he says, to send a message to his Jewish friends: “Not everybody hates you. You are loved.”

The Culture Club frontman, 65, was not nearly prepared for the massive backlash that ensued, which spilled outside of social media into friendships that went back years. Among them was his collaborator Martyn Ware, the founder of New Wave bands like Human League and Heaven 17, who messaged him: “Shame on you.” Related Stories Movies Basque Cinema Expands Its (Rapidly) Growing Footprint in Tallinn TV 'Slow Horses' Actress Ruth Bradley to Be Honored at the Oscar Wilde Awards (Exclusive)

Undeterred, he recently visited Israel and played two sold-out shows at Tel Aviv’s Menora Mivtachim Arena on Nov. 4 and 7. He also met with Israeli president Isaac Herzog and Nova survivors.

With his single now officially released and available on most streaming platforms, Boy George checked in with The Hollywood Reporter on a stop in Chicago.

He opened up about his Israel trip, explained why he used AI to make “We Will Dance Again” and revealed where he stands on the Ed Sheeran/Macklemore controversy that resulted in Sheeran’s entire band walking off of his world tour.

What’s a memory you cherish from the trip to Israel?

One of the sweetest things to me was people from London saying, “Oh, my aunt’s in Israel, can she come and see you? And will you say hello?” And I met a few friends of mine’s families that they haven’t seen for years. And just meeting the Nova survivors, that was probably the highlight of the whole trip, because you suddenly understand why this journey started in the first place.

I saw the Nova exhibition in L.A. It’s a very, very dark exhibit. When you played Israel, didn’t some of the survivors come onstage with you?

Yeah. There was about 13 survivors and also people that lost family. So it was a real mix of people. I think it was really beautiful to let people be seen. And obviously, as a performer, you are uncomfortable because you don’t want to hijack someone else’s experience. You want to show it respect, but at the same time, going to Israel was a massive statement, and I had to be bold about it.

Somebody that reviewed my show said I should have snuck in quietly. I was like, “That wasn’t an option.” And sneaking in quietly right now is not helpful because nobody else is being quiet. So I don’t think it’s the time to be quiet. I think it’s the time to be clear and to be loving. And I’ve made some mistakes in my communication online. There are people I shouldn’t have bothered reacting to.

You’ve said you’ve lost friends over this. Is there anyone you’d name?

Martyn Ware, who was in Human League and Heaven 17. I’ve worked with Martyn. I did a version of “I Wanna Be Your Dog” with him, and we sung onstage together. He came out quite early and said, “Shame on you,” and all of that. I just tweeted back, “You’ve lost that loving feeling. “

Most of them I don’t want to name because I don’t want them to have any more attention than they’ve already had from me.

You’ve said the music industry has been very one-sided on this. Do you find most people in the industry are anti-Israel?

I don’t think they’re anti-Israel. I just think they’re not necessarily informed about history, and it’s become quite trendy to shout certain things out. There’s a lot of pressure to agree with everybody else. Your thought, my thought, are not the deciding factor on what happens in the Middle East. We’ve got nothing to do with it. We’re not going to change it. So what we can do is, in our immediate surroundings, be more loving, be more open, be more kind, respectful, all of those things that you can do.

I was getting messages from friends of mine all over the world. Some people I didn’t even know were Jewish. And I’ve had letters from people. So there is a lot of support out there, but it is scared of itself.

When I saw you speaking up for your Jewish friends on Ireland’s Late Late Show in May, I knew you had something in your heart that needed expressing. I didn’t realize to what extent it would end up expressing itself.

When I decided to make this record, I was sending a message to my friends to say, “Not everybody hates you. You are loved.” And that’s the point I tried to make when I was in Ireland, is that growing up during the punk period, one of my biggest allies was a man called Philip Sallon.

One of the first things he ever said to me was, “You look Irish.” And I said, “I actually am.” And he said, “I’m a Jew and a homosexual.” And that was 1976. And when you’re young and impressionable, you don’t forget those moments. And Philip was a big reason why I got to know so many Jewish people because in London, this one knows that one, that one knows that one.

I can’t sit here and say every single Jewish person I’ve ever met I’ve got on with. Of course I haven’t. But Jewish people are characterful, they’re kind, they’re passionate, they’re funny, they’re bitchy. They’ve got all the qualities that I love.

You said you used AI to make “We Will Dance Again.” What aspect of the song uses AI?

The music I wrote with an AI tool. The melody I [wrote], the words I [wrote] And the reason I used AI was because I didn’t want somebody hanging over my shoulder saying, “Oh, you can’t say that. Oh, that’s too harsh.” And that would’ve happened because I’ve had 40 years of that. Forty years of “That’s too gay, that’s too this, that’s too that, that’s not commercial.”

I make a lot of music. I put out almost 200 tracks in the last two or three years. So I’m always writing. In respect to “We Will Dance Again,” that line, that’s a hook. You hear it straight away. As a writer, you go, “Got it. I got it. OK, there we go.”

I saw a video where you were performing a concert and the music cut out and you kept singing a cappella. I couldn’t tell whether it was intentional sabotage or a technical snafu. What happened?

I think it was a “technical snafu.” I love that word. I don’t think it was deliberate. There was a couple of moments when the mic went and I wasn’t sure whether I’d pulled out the wire. And also the guitar went out in a really pivotal moment, but I kept my cool. We were in the round, so there was nowhere to hide. When you’re in a proscenium situation, you can always dart off into a curtain for a minute, but in this instance, you couldn’t really leave.

What it reminded me of is Ed Sheeran, who went onstage after his band walked out on him and did his show a cappella.

In the moment, you always have to remember that people have paid to see you and that you’re there to perform. You can’t get lost in something, but it can really distract your whole evening. I think it’s really important to go, “No, no, no, I have to get through this.”

Do you have anything else to say about Ed Sheeran in terms of that whole controversy regarding Macklemore being dropped from his tour?

Obviously, the stadium owner, Robert Kraft, I know that he’s Jewish. He spent a long time fighting antisemitism, so he was never going to allow that to carry on where he worked, where he’s an owner.

You have a choice who comes and sits at your dinner table, whether you’re a billionaire or a housewife, you make a choice. And if you’ve cooked the meal and paid for the building, you do have a right as a person, as a Jewish person, to say, “No, you can take your freedom of speech over there and do it on the street.”

I told my editor that “Do You Really Want to Hurt Me” is like a Jewish mother song.

Brad Pitt Wants to Play a James Bond Villain

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The announcement finally arrived this week: Robert Pattinson is James Bond. But before we could even come up for air about Amazon MGM and director Denis Villeneuve’s decision to have arguably the busiest movie star in the business today follow Daniel Craig as 007, the Internet had already moved on. Fans started asking more questions. Who will costar as the next Bond girl? Who’s performing the song? And, most importantly, who would play opposite Pattinson as the villain?

Well, how about Brad Pitt? The F1 star likely didn’t expect a James Bond question when he stepped on the red carpet for the premiere of his latest film, The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth. The spinoff sequel of Quentin Tarantino’s Once Upon a Time... in Hollywood sees Pitt return as stuntman Cliff Booth under the direction of David Fincher, which couldn’t be further from the life of a British MI6 hitman. Still, Pitt was excited to hear for the first time that Pattinson landed the role when a reporter for ExtraTV shared the news. “I love this idea,” Pitt said. “He’s the real deal.”

Would he ever think about playing the villain? Pitt winked at the camera and said, “Robert? Denis? You know what, I’d like to do that. My man Javier [Bardem] did it so well [in Skyfall] It’s tall shoes, but I’d take that on.”

Later down the red carpet, Pitt answered the question again when Complex asked if he had any advice for Pattinson. “Sign me for the villain,” he said, coming around to the idea even more. “Get Denis Villeneuve on the flip phone. Let’s go!”

Elsewhere, Pattinson’s fellow Dune: Part Three costar Florence Pugh is already pitching herself as a potential villain and/or Bond girl. When Villeneuve presented her with an honor at Elle's Women in Hollywood event on Monday, Pugh congratulated the director on finding the next James Bond. “Nice catch,” she said. “Any villains the size of a five-foot-four-inch female?" I’m sure this is just the beginning.

I’ll admit, Pitt wasn’t my first choice for the next Bond villain. If anything, Pattinson was. But I love how enthusiastic he is about the possibility. Now, I kind of feel like I need to see it.

What Is a Thought Made Of? Scientists Reveal Why Human Consciousness May Be Beyond the Reach of AI

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A familiar song can pull you back decades in an instant, and the chemistry behind that rush of memory may hold clues to what separates human minds from AI.

How does the brain turn a collection of memories into thoughts, emotions, and the experience of being conscious? Scientists have spent decades studying how neurons communicate through electrical and chemical signals, yet the physical processes that allow us to recall experiences, attach feelings to them, and give them meaning remain incompletely understood.

A new scientific review suggests that part of the answer may lie in the complex chemistry surrounding brain cells. Rather than viewing memory primarily as a product of neuronal activity, the researchers propose that interactions among neurons, supporting brain cells, and molecules in their surrounding environment could play a fundamental role in shaping thought and conscious experience.

Dr. Gerard Marx of MX Biotech Ltd. and Prof. Chaim Gilon of the Hebrew University of Jerusalem’s Department of Chemistry present their theory in Consciousness, Memory, and Intelligence: A Mechanistic Perspective. Building on earlier research into how experiences might be physically stored in the brain, they examine whether the chemistry of memory could help explain the origins of thought and consciousness.

Their proposal also raises a question about artificial intelligence: If human memory and emotion depend on the chemistry of living cells, can computers ever develop consciousness simply by becoming more powerful?

Scientists have long studied how neurons communicate to form and retrieve memories. But Marx and Gilon suggest that important parts of this process may also occur in the molecular environment surrounding brain cells.

Their model, called the Tripartite Mechanism of Memory, involves three interacting components: brain cells, particularly neurons and astrocytes; the molecular material surrounding those cells; and chemicals such as metal ions, neurotransmitters, and gliotransmitters.

According to the theory, these components could allow the brain to chemically encode and retrieve information. Rather than serving only as a support structure, the environment surrounding brain cells may actively participate in memory storage.

Astrocytes, star-shaped cells that interact closely with neurons, play a central role in the proposed mechanism. The researchers suggest that coordinated activity among these cells, neurons, and surrounding molecules could contribute to how memories are formed and processed.

A familiar song can bring back memories from decades ago, complete with feelings that seemed long forgotten. Such experiences show that remembering involves more than retrieving information.

Marx and Gilon propose that the emotional content of memories may be linked to their chemical encoding. In their model, signaling molecules, including neurotransmitters and gliotransmitters, help connect stored information with the feelings associated with an experience.

The researchers also suggest that retrieving and interpreting these chemically stored memories may contribute to the emergence of thought. In this view, memory is not simply a record of past events but an active process through which the brain assigns meaning to experience.

Computers and brains both store and process information, but the underlying mechanisms differ. While computers rely on electronic hardware, biological memory involves living cells and complex chemical interactions.

Marx and Gilon argue that this distinction could matter for consciousness. AI systems can perform increasingly sophisticated intellectual tasks, but their ability to process information does not establish that they experience thoughts or emotions.

For example, an AI system can identify a familiar song and describe the memories or feelings it might evoke in a person. Whether such a system could actually experience nostalgia is a different question.

The researchers suggest that if consciousness depends partly on the biochemical processes involved in human memory, greater computational power alone may not be sufficient to reproduce it.

The proposed model offers a possible connection between memory, emotion, and thought, but it does not establish how physical processes in the brain produce conscious experience.

The authors acknowledge that consciousness remains poorly understood and do not claim to have demonstrated that AI cannot become conscious.

Instead, their work suggests that investigating the chemical mechanisms of memory could help explain how stored experiences acquire emotional meaning and contribute to thought. Whether those processes are essential for consciousness remains an open question.

Reference: “Consciousness, Memory and Intelligence: A Mechanistic Perspective” by Gerard Marx and Chaim Gilon, 4 August 2026, International Journal of Psychiatry Research. DOI: 10.33425/2641-4317.1251

OpenAI says its AI found 14% gains in AMD’s flagship GPU design

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Some of the model's circuit changes are already in a chip AMD has sent for manufacturing.

OpenAI says its models improved power, performance and area by up to 14% on AMD’s flagship GPU designs, with some changes in a chip sent for manufacturing and single-digit gains on AMD’s next flagship CPU. It also detailed AI-made gains on its own Jalapeño chip and is training a GPT-Synopsys model with Synopsys.

OpenAI says its AI models have improved the design of AMD’s flagship graphics chips, with some changes already in a chip sent for manufacturing. In a blog post on Wednesday, it reported gains of up to 14% in power, performance and area, the three measures chip designers trade off, against AMD’s heavily optimised designs.

The model rewrote the code that describes a chip’s circuits, and used Synopsys software to check each change still worked. OpenAI says this was about a year’s worth of progress in under five months. AMD also confirmed single-digit gains in key parts of its next flagship CPU, according to OpenAI.

OpenAI used the same approach on Jalapeño, its first in-house chip, which went from design to manufacturing in nine months. Changes suggested by its models cut the size of two key parts of the chip by about 13% and 10%, and worked in the finished silicon. Startups are chasing the same idea: Architect Labs raised $24M in June to help companies design custom chips with AI.

OpenAI also tested five generations of its models on designing two small open-source processors. Its newest, GPT-6.2 Astra, scored highest both when improving existing designs and when building a processor from written requirements. OpenAI noted the second test was easier, because models could leave out features the originals had.

Next, OpenAI is training GPT-Synopsys with Synopsys, which is refocusing on AI design, to run the design tools engineers already use. Not everyone is fully convinced yet. OpenAI’s own chip chief, Richard Ho, said last month he still does not fully trust AI design changes, so every one is checked.

Why your fine-tuning job takes longer than the FLOPs say

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Predicting end-to-end fine-tuning time on shared GPU clusters, from 272 production runs.

A preview of our upcoming paper, Predicting End-to-End Fine-Tuning Time for Generative Language Models. The full version will be on arXiv soon.

GPUs are among the most expensive and scarcest resources in AI today. Anyone running a shared cluster has to decide, every day, which jobs go on which GPUs and in what order. That plan is only as good as the estimate of how long each job will take. When a job finishes hours early, GPUs sit idle until the next one is dispatched. When it runs hours late, everything queued behind it slips, experiments wait and delivery dates move.

Better duration estimates turn this into planning instead of guesswork: what runs, when, and on how many GPUs, with less idle capacity in between. Yet the estimate most teams use is a back-of-the-envelope FLOP calculation. It says nine hours, and the job finishes the next afternoon.

We wanted to know how far off that math really is, and whether we could do better before a job is even dispatched. So we took 272 real supervised fine-tuning (SFT) jobs from our production pool of NVIDIA H200 GPUs, about 7,382 GPU-hours in total, and compared analytical estimates against learned predictors.

The short version: the usual FLOP-based estimate explains little of what we observed (R² = 0.31, MAE 12.41 h). A CatBoost regressor that only sees information available before dispatch reaches R² = 0.98, with a mean absolute error of 1.71 h and a median error of 0.51 h in 5-fold cross-validation. The gap comes from two things a closed-form estimate cannot do. The regressor sees more training-specific signals, such as evaluation cadence, checkpoint policy, per-GPU load and fine-tuning regime, and it learns the nonlinear interactions between them from past runs.

For a dense Transformer, training costs roughly 6·P·D floating-point operations, where P is the parameter count and D the number of training tokens. Divide that by what your GPUs can deliver and you get a training-loop time:

Here N_GPU is the number of GPUs, FLOPS_peak is the peak throughput of one GPU from its spec sheet, and MFU (Model FLOPs Utilization) is the fraction of that peak the training actually achieves. Real jobs never run at peak because of memory traffic, communication and kernel inefficiencies, so MFU typically sits somewhere between 30% and 60%, and the right value for a given job is not known in advance.

This is a good description of the optimization loop. A job is more than its loop, though. Before the first step there is environment setup, model download and loading, and tokenization. During training there are periodic evaluations and checkpoint uploads. At the end, the final model goes to object storage. On a multi-tenant cluster, all of it competes with other jobs for network and storage I/O.

These overheads depend on configuration, and they do not scale neatly with workload or GPU count. Two jobs with the same nominal compute can finish hours apart.

To keep the comparison fair, the FLOP baseline we test counts the scheduled evaluation tokens as well as the training tokens, and we sweep MFU over a wide range.

We treat end-to-end duration as a supervised regression problem. Given a feature vector built from the job configuration and model metadata, predict the wall-clock time between the moment the job gets its GPUs and the moment it is done. Queue time is excluded.

We compared Linear Regression, Ridge, XGBoost and CatBoost. Feature selection combined correlation filtering, mutual information and permutation importance across 18 candidate feature sets, followed by a grid search with MAE as the criterion. The final CatBoost model uses 24 features.

Distribution of end-to-end duration across the 272 runs. Mean 23.43 h, median 15.29 h.

All numbers come from stratified 5-fold cross-validation. The first four rows are analytical and involve no fitting.

No single MFU value fixes the FLOP baseline. We swept MFU from 0.25 to 0.65. The best setting, 30%, still leaves a mean error above 12 hours on jobs that last 23 hours on average.

A more detailed analytical model does not close the gap. As a second baseline we took Calculon (Isaev et al., SC '23), an analytical performance model for LLM training from prior work. It builds an explicit layer graph and accounts for compute, memory traffic and inter-device communication. We extended the public implementation to cover LoRA, grouped-query attention, fused attention, bfloat16 and SwiGLU. Its estimates still correspond to a median of only 11% of the observed wall-clock time. That says nothing against Calculon as a step-level tool, which is what it was designed for. It shows how much of a production job happens outside the optimization step.

Linear models help, and then stall. Ridge brings MAE down to 7.23 h. The relationship between configuration and duration is not well captured by a purely linear model.

Gradient boosting does the heavy lifting. CatBoost and XGBoost land within one standard deviation of each other on every metric. We carry CatBoost forward because it has the lowest mean error across the board.

CatBoost's feature importance concentrates in a handful of features. Model and workload variables are there, as expected, but so are evaluation and checkpoint variables. num_eval_data, the total number of samples processed in evaluation over the life of the job, ranks first, followed by num_trainable_params, batch_eval_size_percent, num_all_params and num_gpus*batch_train_percent, with num_params_saved close behind. SHAP values tell the same story: high evaluation volume consistently pushes the predicted time up, while trainable parameter count has a nonlinear effect that only becomes large for the biggest models.

Mean feature importance of the CatBoost model across folds (PredictionValuesChange).

Feature importance is not a causal claim. Still, the pattern lines up with how a job is actually spent. How often you evaluate, how large the evaluation set is and how many checkpoints you persist all shape the wall-clock time, alongside model and dataset scale. These are choices people often make by habit when writing a training config.

One caveat on reading the plots: larger GPU allocations show positive contributions to predicted time. In our data, bigger allocations go with bigger jobs, so num_gpus acts partly as a proxy for job scale. It does not mean adding GPUs makes a job slower.

Looking at all 272 out-of-fold predictions, the median absolute error is 0.51 h. 80% of predictions are within 2.20 h and 90% are within 4.31 h. Broken down by job duration:

Up to 60 hours, the median error stays at about one hour or less. The long tail is where the model struggles, and with only 13 runs above 60 hours we read that as a data limitation.

Out-of-fold absolute error against real duration, colored by duration range. Errors stay low up to 60 h and widen in the sparse tail.

More data should still help. On a fixed 20% holdout, MAE drops from 1.63 h to 1.50 h when going from 85% to 100% of the training partition, with no clear plateau.

In practice, the predictor gives a scheduler a duration it can plan around before a job starts: which jobs to run together, when GPUs will free up, and whether a run will finish in time for its deadline.

What does transfer is the method: overhead-aware features, multicollinearity-aware selection and the comparison protocol. The full paper will report the feature formulas, the selection procedure, the search spaces and the final hyperparameters in full, so the pipeline can be rebuilt on any execution-log dataset with a similar structure.

Next on our list: hardware-specific descriptors and validation on other GPU types, transfer learning for configurations with few observations, other training objectives, and more data on long runs.

The full paper is coming to arXiv soon. Follow the Dharma-AI organization on Hugging Face to be notified when it is out, and leave your questions in the comments below. We would like to hear how you estimate job duration on your own clusters.

Gladstone Capital (GLAD) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Investment income climbed, but markdowns pulled net asset value lower.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 18, 2026 at 1:53PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Gladstone Capital (GLAD -1.96%) reported fiscal year 2026 results highlighted by an 11.7% increase in total investment income and a strategic focus on the lower middle market precision manufacturing sector. Management reported that net investment income remained flat year over year at $2.00 per share as growth in average earning assets was offset by increased financing costs and higher management fees. The company reported a decline in net asset value to $20.77 per share, primarily driven by unrealized depreciation in nonaccrual assets and specific equity positions. Strategic initiatives for the coming fiscal year include returning nonearning investments to earning status and managing leverage within a target range of 1.1 times net asset value.

Operator: Greetings. Welcome to Gladstone Capital Corporation Year-End and Fourth Quarter Earnings Call. [Operator Instructions] Please note, this conference is being recorded. I will now turn the conference over to David Gladstone, Chairman. David, you may begin.

David Gladstone: Thank you. This is David Gladstone, and this is year-end call. We've had good years and good quarters. So everything seems to be fun at the company. We're going to start out with our head of all the kind of connections that we're doing, Catherine Gerkis is the next up. Catherine, go ahead.

Catherine Gerkis: Thanks, David, and good morning, everyone. As David said, this is the earnings conference call for Gladstone Capital for the quarter and fiscal year ended September 30, 2026. Thank you for calling in. Joining me this morning are David Gladstone, Chairman, Bob Marcotte, our Chief Executive Officer; Mike McQuigg, our President; and Nicole Schaltenbrand, our Chief Financial Officer. We're always happy to talk with our shareholders and analysts and to provide an update on the company. I'll begin with a brief disclosure regarding certain regulatory matters. Today's call may include forward-looking statements, which are based on management's estimates, assumptions and projections.

There are no guarantees of future performance, and actual results may differ materially from those expressed or implied in these statements due to various uncertainties, including the risk factors set forth in our SEC filings, which you can find on the Investors page of our website, gladstonecapital.com. We assume no obligation to update any of these statements unless required by law. Please visit our website for a copy of our Form 10-K, earnings press release and investor presentation for more detailed information. Bob and Nicole will refer to a few slides from the presentation this morning. You can also sign up for our e-mail notification service and find information on how to contact our Investor Relations department.

Now I'll turn the call over to Gladstone Capital's Chief Executive Officer, Bob Marcotte.

Robert Marcotte: Thank you, Catherine, and good morning, and thank you all for dialing in. Mike McQuigg, who joined Gladstone in 2015 and became President of Gladstone Capital on October 1, is with us this morning, and we'll join Nicole and I for your questions. I'll cover the highlights for the quarter and the fiscal year, how we invest and cover some of the valuation movements in the portfolio. Nicole will provide some details on the financial results, and I'll conclude with the market, our investment activity and our near-term outlook. Beginning with last quarter's results. Net interest income -- net investment income came in at $11 million or $0.49 per share for the period, in line with the prior quarter.

Investment balances and yields held and our net interest margin was 8.2%. Fee and dividend income of $200,000 was below historical averages, and with a $480,000 incentive fee credit from our adviser, net investment income after preferred dividends covered 100% of our $0.45 regular monthly distributions. For the year, total investment income rose 11.7%, and net investment income of $2 per share covered our regular distributions at 104.5% after preferred dividends. The net asset value declined $0.73 to $20.77 per share on the quarter, $0.18 of which was -- of the decline was the supplemental distribution we paid in September. And the balance from unrealized depreciation of the portfolio, net of a small realized gain.

About 2/3 of the depreciation for the quarter was on loans to 2 existing nonaccrual investments, which had the effect of reducing our nonearning assets to 2.8% of the portfolio fair value. The depreciation of several appreciated equity investments also contributed to the unrealized depreciation for the period, which is not uncommon, as small moves in EBITDA or market multiples can impact our equity positions. I'll get into some of the valuation movements in a few moments. However, I think it's a good time to reiterate the underlying discipline of our investment focus, process and structuring, which has not fundamentally changed. We have been lending to the lower middle market for 25 years.

While the companies we lend to can grow to be relatively large, we do not compete in the broader middle market loan arena. Our borrowers are established growth-oriented businesses with EBITDA of typically under $10 million in origination, and most of them are backed by private equity sponsors. We find the competitive dynamics in the lower middle market to be attractive, as our sources suggest about 80% of buyout transactions are under $100 million. Conversely, most of the private credit inflows are increasingly centered upmarket, as indicated by the 79% of direct lending funds raised in 2026 went to funds over $5 billion according to PitchBook.

And a fund that size is certainly not going to come down to compete with us. So while we operate in the broad deal market, which is outside the strike zone of most of the institutional capital flows, that allows us to be selective and disciplined regarding our credit, secured structure and economic terms. We originate nearly all of our investments ourselves. We look at each one as a business and not as a financial transaction. We do our diligence in much the same manner as an equity investor would, albeit we use the insight to lead the structuring, documentation and act as agent for most of our investments, which include financial maintenance covenants on all our loans.

The teams sourcing and underwriting each investment are responsible for continuing to monitor the investment until it's exited. As a result, we believe we hold very few of the same loans as the other public BDCs, and our credit results depend on our own underwriting and portfolio management. As validation of our approach over the last 10 years on a split-adjusted basis, GLAD's net investment value grew -- per share grew by $3.53, and we paid $18.14 per share in distributions, which is a return of 1.6x the average public BDC over the same period. Turning to how we manage the credit.

We have board observation rights or sit on the board of nearly all of our portfolio companies and receive monthly financials so we can anticipate credit issues and take action sooner. When a business underperforms, we decide what changes might be required and whether capital from us would improve recovery or add to our exposure. Last quarter, that meant advancing capital to 2 of our non-earning companies and electing not to invest further in a third. Net unrealized depreciation came in at $13.6 million for the period. However, the 7 largest decliners accounted for $20.2 million of markdowns, and the rest of the portfolio was up a net $6.6 million.

Delineating the depreciation a bit further, $8.7 million decline was driven by Eegee's and B+T, which have been on nonaccrual. The largest is Eegee's, an Arizona restaurant business we control, and we discussed in the last quarter, revenue was weaker than we anticipated when -- which reduced the value of the business, and we advanced additional capital to support the new management's plans. Unrealized depreciation on the loan was $5.5 million for the quarter. The second is B+T, the telecommunication services company that has been on nonaccrual for more than a year. We elected not to invest further and wrote the remaining $3.2 million of loan value down to 0.

We expect to exit that position in the December quarter with no further impact on our NAV. On the equity side, 3 positions resulted in unrealized depreciation of $7.1 million, which included a dredging contractor, a water well driller in Texas and a health care marketing services business. Earnings at each fell short of plan, but all 3 are performing businesses and 2 of the 3 positions are still valued above our cost. Net of the nonaccrual and equity valuations outlined, the balance of the portfolio continued to perform well with nominal depreciation.

As of the end of the quarter, our nonearning investments were unchanged at 5 companies with a cost basis of $52.9 million or 6% of debt investments at cost and a fair value of $23.8 million or 2.8% of fair value compared with $30.6 million or 3.6% of portfolio at June 30. Workouts take time, and I'm not going to put a date on them, but I can point to our record. Over the last 5 years, our equity investments produced approximately $64.2 million of net gains realized and unrealized, which more than covered the $26.3 million of net losses on our loans. We remain confident in the outlook for these businesses and anticipate their improvement in the coming quarters.

And now I'll turn the call over to Nicole to provide some details of the fund's financial results for the quarter. Nicole?

Nicole Schaltenbrand: Thanks, Bob. Good morning, everyone. During the September quarter, total interest income rose $100,000 to $24.4 million as average earning assets were largely unchanged, while the weighted average yield on our interest-bearing portfolio declined slightly to 11.7% for the period. Total investment income was $24.6 million compared with $24.5 million in the prior quarter. PIK income for the quarter declined to $1.4 million or 5.7% of interest income. Total expenses were largely unchanged at $13.6 million versus the prior quarter as interest expense rose $300,000 with a full quarter of the 7% notes issued in June, net of lower average line of credit borrowings, and net base management fees rose $200,000.

These increases were offset by a $600,000 decline in net incentive fees, which includes a $500,000 incentive fee credit for the quarter. Net investment income for the quarter was $11 million or $0.49 per share. After $900,000 of preferred dividends, it was $10.2 million or 100% of the regular monthly distributions declared on common shares. The $0.18 supplemental distribution paid in September was declared separately from the regular monthly rate and is not part of that coverage. With respect to net asset value, as laid out on Slide 9 of our investor presentation, we began the quarter at $21.50 per share. Net investment income was $0.49 less $0.04 of preferred dividend, covered our $0.45 of regular distributions.

The supplemental distribution reduced NAV by $0.18, and net unrealized depreciation reduced it by $0.48 on debt investments and $0.13 on equity investments, which is partially offset by $0.06 of realized gains, which brings us to $20.77 as of September 30. The net decrease in net assets resulting from operations was $2.2 million or $0.10 per share as impacted by the unrealized depreciation covered by Bob earlier. For the fiscal year, total investment income rose $10.5 million or 11.7% to $99.6 million as average earning assets rose $121.9 million or 17.9%, and while the weighted average yield declined to 11.9% from 12.7% in the prior year, driven mainly by lower SOFR rates.

Total expenses rose by about the same amount with interest expense up $5 million on higher average line of credit borrowings and the change in mix of our debt. Net management fees were up $2.9 million on higher average and higher average assets and net incentive fees were up $1.9 million as the incentive fee credit was $500,000 this year compared to $2.3 million in the prior year. Net investment income for the year was $45.1 million or $2 per share compared with $45.2 million or $2.02 per share last year. After $2.6 million of preferred dividends, net investment income covered 104.5% of our regular distributions for the year.

With $11.5 million of net unrealized depreciation for the year, the net increase in net assets resulting from operations was $32 million or $1.42 per share. We paid $1.98 per share in regular and supplemental distributions for the year and NAV per share declined $0.57. Moving over to the balance sheet. As of September 30, total assets rose to $973 million, consisting of $956 million in investments at fair value and $17.7 million in cash and other assets. Liabilities were $453 million as of September 30, up from $439 million at June 30, with the increase in total debt.

Borrowings under our $385 million line of credit were $188 million as of the end of the quarter, and the remaining balance of our liabilities consist primarily of $149.5 million of 5.875% convertible notes due in October 2030, $60 million of 7% notes due December 2029 and $50 million of 3.75% notes due in May. Availability under the line of credit was approximately $178 million at the end of the quarter. The line is our only floating rate borrowing at 42% of principal debt, while about 85% of our debt investments carry floating rate. During the quarter, we also issued $6.8 million of our 6.25% Series A preferred stock, which has a principal balance of $57 million.

As of September 30, net assets declined $16.4 million since June 30 to $469.3 million. Debt to equity, including our preferred stock, was 1.08x compared with 1x at June 30. On principal debt alone, it was 0.95x compared to 0.9x at June 30. With respect to distributions, monthly distributions for November and December will be $0.15 per common share which is an annual run rate of $1.80 per share. The Board will meet again in January to determine the monthly distribution to common stockholders for the following quarter. Our accumulated spillover was approximately $5.5 million or $0.24 per share as of September 30. And now I'll turn it back to Bob for the market and our outlook.

Robert Marcotte: Thank you, Nicole. Turning to the market. Deal activity in the larger market swings with the rates in the economy, and it's been flat to slowing for most of the year. The lower middle market is less affected as its deal flow is often driven by family-owned or founder-owned businesses changing hands. As outlined in our investor presentation posted last night, the lower middle market continues to see relatively stable purchase multiples and modest leverage in the range of 2.9 to 3.7x EBITDA over the past 2 years as well as a healthy pricing premium on borrowers with less than $10 million of EBITDA compared to the more competitive middle market.

Based on the modest leverage profile of our investments, we occasionally see smaller commercial banks in the mix, but their structural inflexibility and responsiveness can be an impediment. To the extent that we might see other unitranche competitors for attractive PE-sponsored situations, where appropriate, we've been successful in winning the deals by bringing in senior lenders to blend down the overall loan rate and continuing to agent and manage the credit as we have historically done. Short-term rates moved up 0.25 point in this mid-September time frame. So most of the effect on our earnings starts in the December quarter.

With most of our loans at floating rates and more than half of our borrowings fixed, this increase and any further movement would serve to enhance our net investment income. Turning to our investment activity. We reviewed about 500 opportunities in the fiscal year just ended and closed 19 transactions. We invested $309 million for the year, including $171 million in 11 new portfolio companies. With repayments down to $210 million from $352 million in the prior year, the portfolio grew by $97 million. Fundings last quarter totaled $84 million and included 2 new investments totaling $33 million and $51 million on the quarter were additional advances to existing portfolio companies.

Both of the new investments are precision manufacturing businesses where order backlogs have been strong. In the aggregate, our originations last quarter had an average leverage of 3.2x EBITDA, 48% loan-to-value at entry and a spread of 730 basis points over SOFR. Exits and repayments came in at $71 million, so net originations were $13 million in the quarter. The 2 exits were legacy energy-related investments that we had anticipated. With respect to the portfolio, first lien debt and total debt investments came in at 74% and 90% of the portfolio cost.

As our leverage has moved up, we've become more judicious about where we are allocating new capital and prioritizing strongly performing portfolio companies that benefit from the scale associated with additional capacity or acquisitions. Since the end of the quarter, we funded $6.9 million in an existing portfolio company to fund an attractive acquisition, and we'll continue to manage our pipeline of late-stage deals generally in line with the near-term repayments and liquidity events. In reflecting on our recently concluded fiscal '26 and the outlook for the next quarter or 2, I'd like to leave you with the following: Total investment income and portfolio each grew more than 11% this year.

But with lower rates, higher financing costs and higher net management fees, net investment income was about flat. We are working to return our non-earning investments to earning status or recover and deploy this capital to scale our earnings as we manage our balance sheet leverage in the range of 1.1x NAV. We reset the regular distribution to $0.15 a month in October 2025 and net investment income after preferred dividends has covered each quarter since.

Going forward, we expect to continue to support the dividend coverage through a combination of fee and dividend income, monetizing equity assets to reinvest in earning assets, the recovery of non-earning loans and we'll consider incentive fee waivers as the manager has done in the past. With the assets we added this year and the recent increase in short-term rates, which equates to about $0.06 a share in NII lift, we expect to continue to sustain our coverage. We are an investment-driven culture, and our priority for fiscal '27 is to improve our net investment income per share while continuing to deliver top quarter economic returns to our shareholders, which they have enjoyed for the past decade or more.

Operator, could you please tell our callers how they can ask their questions?

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Our first question is from Mickey Schleien with Clear Street.

Mickey Schleien: Bob, I just wanted to follow up on your comments about the pipeline. There seems to be a consensus building that M&A activity could pick up next year. But from my perspective, there's still a lot of uncertainty in terms of what the Fed is going to do. There's also uncertainty at the macroeconomic level and at the political level. So I'd be really interested in understanding what your thesis is on deal flow for next year.

Robert Marcotte: Mickey, there are obviously mixed signals. I say that the forward curve for SOFR is going up. That's not a good thing for buyouts. There's certainly been some measure of volatility around economic conditions and tariffs and the like. That said, I think the recent political events have created a scenario where there's more likely to be a roadblock or a do-nothing environment from a regulatory perspective. So I do think that some of these portfolio companies have gotten long in the tooth and it's time to sell and the LP pressure on the PE funds is pretty significant. When we look at the sale transactions, we really look at both the refinancing transactions and sales transactions.

In the refinancing environment, a large number of our companies have really grown. I mean when I said we take -- we start under $10 million, we've got companies that are $30 million, $40 million in EBITDA. So they have really scaled. The clear appreciation of their multiples in the market has changed, and these tend to be growth-oriented businesses. So what we see in a more challenged environment are companies that have grown, that have delivered, that have good growth outlook are going to continue to trade. So that's kind of one. Two, based on the scale, the market opportunities are there for them to refinance. Obviously, we tend to hold to our starting terms when we originate assets.

And if EBITDA is 2 or 3 or 4x what we entered at, there's plenty of folks upmarket that will leverage that at a lower rate or at a higher multiple. I do think there are a number of plays in our business and sectors where growth rate will just dictate acquisition and larger sponsors are going to want to get into that business. I'll give you a couple of examples. We have a lot of precision manufacturing companies in our business. These folks are seeing significant continuation to bring business back to the states.

They're seeing huge amounts of defense and aerospace spend and contracts that are going to grow dramatically and consolidating to become a larger and more encompassed and capable competitor in that business is critical to the primes to continue to supply the growth in some of those end markets. So we would expect the activity to be somewhat tied to certain growth sectors. We're seeing that in some of our businesses even at the scale that we are, and we would expect that to continue.

I don't think the financial arbitrage, the buying at a high level of leverage at an elevated multiple is a game that is going to be particularly successful in 2027, but that's not where we see the M&A market. Our companies start at roughly 7 to 7.5 turns on a multiple basis. When they grow to $20 million or $30 million of EBITDA, they will trade for 9x, 12x depending on the underlying industry. So the core drivers to the sale transactions for our business are very different than the broader upmarket environment, which I think is going to continue to be challenged by the flow of funds.

The net redemptions in the -- in the private BDCs is certainly not creating a particularly robust environment for financing in that sector. So bottom line is I think the lower middle market is going to continue to trade. That's where most of the transactions happen. And I feel pretty good about where our companies are in seeing that liquidity. And as I said, we'll continue to monitor that and driving our investment activities going forward.

Mickey Schleien: And Bob, if I could follow up with one question on what you just said. I mean your new originations came in at attractive spreads during the quarter that you just reported. But you've also talked about repayment and refinancing activity as your portfolio companies grow. Does that pose too much of a headwind? Or do you see opportunities to stabilize or perhaps even improve your portfolio yields, notwithstanding what the forward curve -- I don't know what the interest rates are going to be a year from now. The forward curve is notoriously wrong. Just big picture, what are you thinking about portfolio yields?

Robert Marcotte: Mickey, it's an interesting question. I would say I think about it 2 ways. The refinancing activity, yes, I don't believe that people are going to be able to dramatically reduce their cost of funds, but that's not the only reason people would consider refinancings. They might be coming to the end of the maturity, they need to extend. They might take a lesser upfront -- a rate that is probably not as attractive, but it's cheaper than where we are. And they might look for a distribution so they could share that money with their LPs in order to provide the liquidity that they're screaming for given the age of some of the companies in these portfolios.

So the refinancing activity may be more leverage driven than rate driven as part of their motivation. As it relates to our improvement of our portfolio yields, one thing that we do not do is we do not do financial transactions in and of themselves. We are not an opportunistic lender. We are not looking for turnaround situations. We are not looking to juice our yields.

The fact that we are putting our investments out in low 3s on a senior secured basis and delivering spreads in the low 7s, I wouldn't want to push that too much because we are going to be taking on credit quality, and we may be moving down the capital stack and increase our risk. So overall, I would say we're probably at the top end of our yield. The only thing that we might do is maybe we do more first out facilities, we're bringing a bank in. We can get a little bit of better pricing off the back end of those situations.

But I wouldn't want to chase yield to materially increase our leverage or credit risk profile, which is core to our business. The last piece I would say is we really like attractive growth businesses. I'd rather finance at an attractive rate, a growth-oriented business than stretch yield because in many cases, we will invest in the equity of that company, too, and we will get a lift on the equity side. As my comments said, we've been very successful in realizing gains on our equity, and we've got a number that we are hoping in 2027 will come to fruition. So good companies also bring equity upside for our co-investments.

And I'd rather do that than chase a turnaround situation where that might not be as clear.

Robert Marcotte: The average -- weighted average leverage in the portfolio today is 3.7x and the weighted average fixed charge coverage is 2.5x.

Erik Zwick: Can you guys still hear me? Sorry, I'm traveling today, having some reception issues.

Erik Zwick: Okay. Great. I guess the only other question I had today, just looking at some of the fair value changes, and thanks for the ones that you addressed in the prepared comments. Curious if you had any commentary you could add on Dutch Gold Honey, which saw a nice appreciation in the quarter.

Robert Marcotte: Dutch Gold, there are some people that would ask questions about consumer trends. Dutch Gold is one of the largest distributors of honey in the Eastern United States. It was bought at a very fairly low multiple. The business did not fall off as much as was expected. In fact, it went the other way, and it's continued to increase as part of the transition from the founding family that have been owning the business for 75 years. They had not been investing in the plant. The sponsor that came in that we assisted in the financing pulled -- laid out a very detailed capital expansion plan to increase their capacity and grow their customer base.

They have executed on that and beginning to pull in additional revenues. So not only did it not go down, but it's dramatically expanding. And the multiples in food and food service-related businesses, particularly around healthy, attractive natural ingredients like honey, which is being used increasingly as a substitute in a variety of ways, it continues to grow. So low multiple expansion, new customers, and growth and exit multiple appreciation are all combining to a very attractive framework here.
